Dimension Reduction via Sum-of-Squares and Improved Clustering Algorithms for Non-Spherical Mixtures
Prashanti Anderson paanders@csail.mit.edu MIT Mitali Bafna mitalib@cs.washington.edu University of Washington Rares-Darius Buhai rares-darius.buhai@epfl.ch EPFL Pravesh K. Kothari kothari@cs.princeton.edu Princeton University David Steurer dsteurer@inf.ethz.ch ETH Zurich

Abstract
We develop a new approach for clustering non-spherical (i.e., arbitrary component covariances) Gaussian mixture models via a subroutine, based on the sum-of-squares method, that finds a low-dimensional separation-preserving projection of the input data. Our method gives a non-spherical analog of the classical dimension reduction, based on singular value decomposition, that, among several other applications, forms a key component of the celebrated spherical clustering algorithm of Vempala and Wang [ 46 ] . As applications, we obtain an algorithm to (1) cluster an arbitrary total-variation separated mixture of k k centered (i.e., zero-mean) Gaussians with n ≥ poly ⁡ ( d ) ​ f ​ ( w min − 1 ) n\geq\mathrm{poly}(d)f(w_{\min}^{-1}) samples and poly ⁡ ( n ) \mathrm{poly}(n) time, and (2) cluster an arbitrary total-variation separated mixture of k k Gaussians with identical but arbitrary unknown covariance with n ≥ d O ⁡ ( log ⁡ w min − 1 ) ​ f ​ ( w min − 1 ) n\geq d^{O(\log w_{\min}^{-1})}f(w_{\min}^{-1}) samples and n O ⁡ ( log ⁡ w min − 1 ) n^{O(\log w_{\min}^{-1})} time. Here, w min w_{\min} is the minimum mixing weight of the input mixture, and f f does not depend on the dimension d d . Our algorithms naturally extend to tolerating a dimension-independent fraction of arbitrary outliers. Before this work, the techniques in the state-of-the-art non-spherical clustering algorithms needed d O ⁡ ( k ) ​ f ​ ( w min − 1 ) d^{O(k)}f(w_{\min}^{-1}) samples and time for clustering such mixtures. Our results may come as a surprise in the context of the d Ω ⁡ ( k ) d^{\Omega(k)} statistical query and sum-of-squares lower bounds [ 21 , 24 ] for clustering non-spherical Gaussian mixtures. While these results are usually thought to rule out d o ⁡ ( k ) d^{o(k)} cost algorithms for the problem, our results show that the lower bounds can in fact be circumvented for a remarkably general class of Gaussian mixtures.



1
Introduction

In this work, we revisit the problem of clustering a high-dimensional Gaussian mixture model.
Specifically, we are interested in algorithms that take i.i.d. samples drawn from a mixture of
k

d
-dimensional Gaussian distributions
\sum_{i\leq k}w_{i}N(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
as input, where
k\ll d
.
The goal is to output a good clustering of the data: partitioning the points based on the component that generated them, with at most
1\%
misclassified points.
Such a goal is statistically possible only if the component Gaussians are pairwise well-separated, i.e., have a total variation distance of at least
1-O(w_{\min})
(or “overlap”
\ll w_{\min}
) between them.
1
1

1

See
[
6
,
16
]
for a detailed discussion.

The Gaussian mixture model (GMM) has a history beginning more than a century ago in the work of Pearson
[
43
]
.
In the last 50 years, the model has been extensively utilized in applications in science and engineering.
Starting with
[
15
]
, it has become a central model studied in algorithmic statistics.
And, recently, an exciting line of work has even found deep connections between the hardness of clustering non-spherical Gaussian mixture models and lattice problems pivotal to modern cryptography
[
11
,
28
]
.
From an algorithm design perspective, the problem of learning GMMs has long served as a benchmark for provable techniques in algorithmic statistics.
Indeed, advances for the problem over the years are closely linked to the development of new broadly applicable tools in statistical learning, such as dimension reduction
[
46
,
9
]
, the random projection method
[
33
,
42
]
, the method of moments
[
33
,
42
]
, tensor methods
[
31
,
27
]
, outlier-filters
[
22
]
, and the sum-of-squares method
[
37
,
29
,
16
,
6
]
.

In his pioneering work, Dasgupta
[
15
]
began the quest for provably efficient algorithms for Gaussian mixture models by focusing on the case of clustering
spherical
(i.e., when all
\Sigma_{i}
s are scalings of
I_{d}
) mixtures.
This quest
[
45
,
1
]
culminated in algorithms to cluster mixtures with a dimension-independent pairwise component separation
[
46
]
.
Their work relied on a
separation-preserving projection
to a low-dimensional subspace based on singular value decomposition.
The quantitative separation requirement was substantially improved to near-optimal in recent years
[
22
,
37
,
29
,
39
]
, and the resulting algorithms also have
outlier-robust
analogs that tolerate a constant fraction of arbitrary outliers
[
17
,
38
]
.

The
non-spherical
case (i.e., arbitrary
\Sigma_{i}
s) has proved more challenging.
The first clustering algorithm for such mixtures formed a crucial subroutine in the celebrated works
[
9
,
42
]
that gave the first polynomial-time algorithms for learning an arbitrary (potentially non-clusterable) GMM.
Their work required running time and sample complexity
(d/w_{\min})^{k^{\mathrm{poly}(k)}}
.
Motivated by the goal of developing outlier-robust algorithms, a recent sequence of works
[
6
,
16
,
40
,
3
]
improved this bound to
d^{O(k)}f(w_{\min}^{-1})
using a new approach based on connections to efficient certificates for concentration and anti-concentration properties of Gaussian distributions.
All these algorithms require
d^{\Omega(k)}
time and samples, which is in stark contrast to the best-known algorithm for clustering spherical mixtures
[
39
]
that runs in time
d^{O(1)}
.
In fact, the statistical query lower bound of
[
21
]
and the sum-of-squares lower bound of
[
24
]
suggest that this cost is likely necessary!

A sub-exponential algorithm for GMMs?

At first glance, this might seem like the final word on the problem — we have algorithms with a cost that aligns perfectly with the lower bounds in various models.
But a deeper dive suggests fascinating twists hiding just beneath the surface.

The hardness results above are based on the
parallel pancakes
construction that starts with a
1
-dimensional mixture of
k
Gaussians which matches the standard Gaussian distribution in its first
\Omega(k)
moments
[
21
]
.
The “hard” mixture is then obtained by
planting
the
1
-D construction in a random direction (with all orthogonal directions being standard Gaussian in all components).
Importantly, the resulting hard family of mixture models has (1) components of exponentially small weight, i.e.,
w_{\min}\sim 2^{-k}
, and (2) non-zero means.

What happens if we restrict ourselves to mixture models where the minimum mixing weight,
w_{\min}
, is at least
1/\mathrm{poly}(k)
?
Or if all the components are centered?
These are natural and well-studied settings in their own right, capturing problems such as mixtures of linear regressions and subspace clustering (covariances with distinct range spaces)
[
14
,
23
]
.

Known algorithmic techniques do not appear to have the power to exploit such natural structures of the mixture components.
Indeed, the clustering algorithms in
[
6
,
16
]
need
d^{\mathrm{poly}(k)}
time and samples even for equiweighted (i.e.,
w_{\min}=1/k
) mixtures with centered components.
2
2

2

A concrete GMM where such methods need
d^{\Omega(k)}
samples is the
mixture of hyperplanes
setting:
\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i}N(0,I-v_{i}v_{i}^{\top})
for arbitrary but distinct unit vectors
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This is a special case of the more general problem of subspace clustering where the covariances are arbitrary projection matrices for which there was no known
d^{o(k)}
time algorithm before this work.

Technically, this is because the total variation separation between two Gaussian distributions
N(0,\Sigma_{1})
and
N(0,\Sigma_{2})
can arise due to the presence of a single direction
v
such that
v^{\top}\Sigma_{1}v/v^{\top}\Sigma_{2}v\gg 1
.
This setting of
spectral separation
in both these works is tackled via certificates of
anti-concentration
, and the best-known techniques
[
35
,
44
,
5
]
for such certificates necessarily incur a time and sample complexity of
d^{\Omega(k)}
.
A recent work of
[
12
]
, also motivated by the question above, showed a weak
partial clustering
algorithm that requires
d^{O(\log w_{\min}^{-1})}
time and samples for Gaussian mixtures with identical covariances.
Their weak partial clustering,
3
3

3

Usually partial clustering algorithms give a near-laminar partition of the data – i.e., every component is almost entirely on one side of the partition and each side has at least one component. In the weak partial clustering guaranteed in
[
12
]
, the partition created is non-laminar and can split all but two clusters arbitrarily.
however, is only guaranteed to separate the samples from two of the components, and is allowed to split the samples from other components arbitrarily, so they cannot iterate to obtain a full clustering.

To summarize, our current algorithmic techniques cannot exploit arguably natural additional structure in non-spherical Gaussian mixtures.
At the same time, our hardness results, based as they are on essentially a single family of hard examples,
4
4

4

One exception that is
not
based on the parallel pancakes construction is the family of hard examples given in
[
20
]
, which shows a quasi-polynomial lower bound for statistical query algorithms that learn mixtures of bounded-covariance GMMs with
\mathrm{polylog}(k)
separation with respect to the
largest
eigenvalue of the component covariance matrices.
do not imply any barriers for such settings.
In this work, we develop a new approach that leads to outlier-robust algorithms for non-spherical mixtures with zero means or identical unknown covariances that substantially improve on the best-known algorithms (even in the setting without outliers).

Our results

In this paper, we present a natural non-spherical analog of the influential dimension reduction technique of
[
46
]
, which gave the first algorithm for clustering spherical mixtures with dimension-independent separation requirements.
Specifically, we show an efficient algorithm based on a new sum-of-squares SDP relaxation that identifies a low-dimensional projection of the input mixture, preserving the total variation separation between at least one pair of initial components.
Unlike the method in
[
12
]
, identifying this low-dimensional subspace allows us to partially cluster (i.e., find a cluster-preserving partition of the data) and iterate while accumulating only a small error.

As applications, we obtain new algorithms for outlier-robust clustering of well-separated
5
5

5

Our separation requirement is satisfied by all mixtures in which every pair of components has total variation distance at least
1-f(w_{\min})
for a monotone
f
. All existing results on clustering mixtures of non-spherical Gaussians require a similar separation.

centered Gaussian mixtures and mixtures with the same (albeit unknown and non-spherical) covariances. Our results significantly improve on all prior works in the high-dimensional setting when
d\gg k,w_{\min}^{-1}
.

The first setting we consider is clustering well-separated centered Gaussian mixtures. Specifically, we prove:

Theorem 1.1

(Main theorem 1, see
Theorem

5.2
for full version)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a mixture of
k
centered Gaussians with minimum weight
w_{\min}
in which every pair of components is well-separated.
6
6

6

Like prior works on clustering non-spherical mixtures
[
16
,
6
]
, we need the separation between every pair of components to be
1-f(w_{\min})
in total variation distance for a monotone
f
.

Also let
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
be a distribution satisfying
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathcal{M})\leq\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon\ll w_{\min}
.
Then there exists an algorithm that takes as input independent samples from
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and outputs a clustering such that in each cluster at most a
O(kw_{\min}^{-1}\max\{\varepsilon,k^{-k}\})
-fraction of points are misclassified. The sample complexity and running time of the algorithm is
d^{O(1)}f(w_{\min}^{-1})
.
7
7

7

It suffices to take
f(x)=\exp(\exp(\exp(\exp(\tilde{O}(x^{2})))))
.

Observe that the exponent of the dimension in the cost above is a
fixed constant completely independent of
k
! This is in contrast to the best-known results
[
16
,
6
,
3
]
, that necessarily incur a
d^{\Omega(k)}
running time and sample complexity even for zero-mean Gaussian mixtures.

Subspace clustering

As an immediate corollary (see
Corollary

5.4
), we obtain an outlier-robust algorithm for Gaussian
subspace clustering
(with arbitrary dimensional subspaces) that runs in time and samples
d^{O(1)}f(w_{min}^{-1})
.
There is a vast body of empirical work on subspace clustering where, roughly speaking, the goal is to find the constituent subspaces in data generated from a union of subspaces.
Our corollary applies to the Gaussian model (studied in
[
14
]
for the special case of subspaces of co-dimension 1).
Formally, we are given a sample from a mixture of zero-mean Gaussian distributions with each covariance restricted to a distinct subspace.
The total variation distance between
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma^{\prime})
is
1
whenever the range spaces of
\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}
are different.
Thus, our theorem above is applicable. While there have been improved algorithms for the special case of subspaces with low dimension or co-dimension (e.g., mixture of hyperplanes
[
14
,
23
]
), to the best of our knowledge, only
d^{\mathrm{poly}(k)}
sample and time algorithms
[
4
,
3
]
were known in general prior to our work.

The second setting we consider is clustering well-separated mixture models where all components have identical but unknown and arbitrary covariance.
This setting was studied recently in the work of Buhai and Steurer
[
12
]
, who gave a
partial clustering
algorithm, with time and sample complexity
d^{O(\log w_{\min}^{-1})}
, for the problem that splits the input sample into two groups so that each side of the partition contains 99% of the samples from at least one component.
Their algorithm, however, can split other components arbitrarily across the two groups.
Hence, one cannot iterate their partial clustering subroutine to find a full clustering of the data.
This limitation appears inherent to their approach.
Here, we give a
d^{O(\log w_{\min}^{-1})}f(w_{\min}^{-1})
samples and
d^{O(\log^{2}w_{\min}^{-1})}f(w_{\min}^{-1})
time algorithm to cluster such mixtures.
Our algorithm builds on a result of
[
12
]
showing that a same-covariance mixture cannot match too many moments of a standard Gaussian; see Section
2
and specifically Section
2.2
for a technical overview of the algorithm.

Theorem 1.2

(Main theorem 2, see
Theorem

6.2
for full version)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a mixture of
k
identical-covariance Gaussians with minimum weight
w_{\min}
in which every pair of components is well-separated.
Also let
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
be a distribution satisfying
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},\mathcal{M})\leq\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon\ll w_{\min}
.
Then there exists an algorithm that takes as input independent samples from
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
and outputs a clustering such that in each cluster at most a
O(kw_{\min}^{-1}\max\{\varepsilon,k^{-k}\})
-fraction of points are misclassified, as long as every pair of components in the input mixture is well-separated.
The sample complexity of the algorithm is
d^{O(\log w_{\min}^{-1})}f(w_{\min}^{-1})
and running time is
d^{O(\log^{2}w_{\min}^{-1})}f(w_{\min}^{-1})
.
8
8

8

It suffices to take
f(x)=\exp(\exp(\exp(\exp(\tilde{O}(x^{2})))))
.

Remark 1.3
.

Following the upload of a preprint of our paper,
[
18
]
showed a
d^{\Omega(\log k)}
statistical query lower bound for the problem considered in
Theorem

1.2
. In fact, their lower bound holds in the special case when all mixing weights are equal and there are no outliers (
\varepsilon=0
). Thus, in the statistical query model, one cannot improve the query complexity to
d^{O(1)}f(w_{\min}^{-1})
, proving a separation between the centered Gaussians setting in
Theorem

1.1
and the identical-covariance Gaussians setting in
Theorem

1.2
.

Our key idea: dimension reduction via sum-of-squares

A key component of our algorithms is a subroutine for finding a projection of the mixture to a subspace of dimension independent of the underlying dimension
d
that still maintains the pairwise separation between at least one pair of components. Let us explain this key idea through our first result on zero-mean non-spherical mixtures of Gaussians. Let us first recall the classical and celebrated idea of Vempala and Wang that accomplished such a step in the context of spherical Gaussian mixtures.

Given a spherical mixture, say,
\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},I)
, the second moment of the mixture is
\sum_{i}w_{i}\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}+I
. If we could project the mixture onto the
k
-dimensional subspace spanned by the means
\mu_{i}
s, we could maintain all the pairwise
\ell_{2}
separations between the component means (and thus maintain the total variation distance) while drastically reducing the dimensionality of the problem to
k\ll d
. The span of
\mu_{i}
s can be easily computed by noting that the second moment of the input mixture minus the identity is
\sum_{i}w_{i}\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}
. While an outlier-robust variant of this
[
29
,
37
,
22
]
requires more care, this idea immediately gives a dimension reduction subroutine in the setting without any outliers.

Now, let us attempt a natural generalization to the non-spherical case when the input mixture is
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{i})
for unknown
\Sigma_{i}
s. The pairwise total variation separation between the components is now governed by the spectral norm
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{-1}\Sigma_{j}\right\rVert_{2}
(or the relative Frobenius norm
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}-I\right\rVert_{F}
, see
[
16
,
6
]
, but this type of separation can be tackled by ideas in prior works). A natural idea would be to find the span of the
\Sigma_{i}
s and then project the second tensor powers of the data points onto this span. However, the natural analog that relies on the fourth-moment tensor (the second moment of the second tensor powers of the data points), tempting as it may appear,
provably fails
,
even for a mixture of two Gaussians
. The key difficulty is that the data only gives access to the
symmetrized
moment tensor. That is, the data allows us to compute
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal{M}}[x^{\otimes 4}]=\operatorname{Sym}(\sum_{i}w_{i}\Sigma_{i}^{\otimes 2})
, where
\operatorname{Sym}
averages its argument over the symmetries of a fourth-order tensor. The
(a,b,c,d)
-th entry of the resulting moments is then
\sum_{i}w_{i}(\Sigma_{i}(a,b)\Sigma_{i}(c,d)+\Sigma_{i}(a,c)\Sigma_{i}(b,d)+\Sigma_{i}(a,d)\Sigma_{i}(b,c))
. This “mixing up” of the four modes makes the resulting fourth-moment tensor, when viewed as a matrix, a
high-rank matrix
(as opposed to rank
k
). And, in fact, recovery is provably impossible, even if
\Sigma_{i}
s are “smoothed” (see Lemma A.2 in
[
2
]
).

A more sophisticated idea is to find a small-dimensional subspace that is guaranteed to contain at least one “direction of separation” — a vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
v^{\top}\Sigma_{i}v\gg v^{\top}\Sigma_{j}v
for some
i,j
that is guaranteed to exist when the components are spectrally separated. To illustrate, consider the special case when
\Sigma_{i}=I-R_{i}
for some low-rank projection matrix
R_{i}
. Then, any
v
in the subspace associated with
R_{i}
is a direction of separation. Of course, in general, while we can use techniques in prior works to ensure that
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I\right\rVert_{F}\leq\mathrm{poly}(k,1/w_{min})
, we cannot actually assume that
\Sigma_{i}
s are low-rank perturbations as in the above ideal case. Nevertheless, this idealized case already helps us showcase the technical issues we will tackle via our new dimension reduction approach. By estimating the fourth
Hermite
moment tensor (which can be obtained by combining the first four raw moment tensors), we can get our hands on
T_{4}=\operatorname{Sym}(\sum_{i}w_{i}R_{i}^{\otimes 2})
. This now appears quite promising since the
R_{i}
s have low rank, and one may expect to “read” off a
v
by simply looking at, say, the canonical flattening of
T_{4}
and taking the top eigenvector. But, unfortunately, as Lemma A.2 from
[
2
]
shows, there can be “cancellations” caused by the symmetrization operation that could, in principle, force that no
v
is close to the top subspace of this matrix.

Sum-of-Squares approach to find
spherical
directions

Our key idea to escape these issues is to develop a new approach to find the
spherical
directions in the input Gaussian mixture. These are directions
v
such that
v^{\top}\Sigma_{i}v\approx\left\lVert v\right\rVert_{2}^{2}
. For every
\Sigma_{i}
, there is a
d-f(k)
dimensional subspace such that all vectors in this subspace are such spherical directions. However, note that the set of spherical directions is not a linear subspace but a complicated semi-algebraic set. Nevertheless, we show that a natural sum-of-squares relaxation can recover spherical directions and, with some augmentation, also help find a direction that is
not
in such a spherical set. We describe our new dimension reduction approach in detail in the technical overview that follows.

While we focused this discussion on zero-mean mixtures, we note that similar issues arise in recovering directions of separation even in the case of unknown but identical-covariance mixtures, as in our second result. Indeed, this is a key difficulty encountered in the prior work
[
12
]
that dealt with such mixtures. It turns out that our dimension reduction subroutine naturally applies and allows us to recover directions of separation even in that setting.

We believe that this subroutine will likely find more applications in statistical estimation.
The next section explains the key ideas behind this subroutine and how it applies to clustering mixtures of Gaussians.

2
Technical Overview

To present our key technical ideas, we focus on the case of equiweighted centered mixtures of Gaussians (without outliers) as the main example and explain how our ideas apply to the identical covariance case at the end.

The certifiable anti-concentration bottleneck

Before discussing our new approach, we explain the key bottleneck in prior approaches to clustering
[
6
,
16
]
.
We note that the works
[
40
,
3
]
use a clustering step
[
6
,
16
]
as a subroutine.

To begin with, let us translate total variation distance into a more directly interpretable parameter distance bound.
Recall (see
[
6
,
16
]
) that if two Gaussian distributions
N(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1})
and
N(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{2})
are separated by a total variation distance of
1-\exp(-O(\Delta^{2}\log\Delta))
for some parameter
\Delta>0
, then they must be
\Delta
-separated in
parameter distance
as defined below:

Definition 2.1

(Parameter distance)
.

We say that two distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with means
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
and positive definite covariances
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}
are
\Delta
-separated in parameter distance if at least one of the following conditions holds for
\left\{i,j\right\}=\left\{1,2\right\}
:

1.

Mean separation:
\exists v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},v\rangle^{2}>\Delta^{2}\cdot v^{\top}(\Sigma_{i}+\Sigma_{j})v
,

2.

Spectral separation:
\exists v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
v^{\top}\Sigma_{i}v>\Delta^{2}\cdot v^{\top}\Sigma_{j}v
,

3.

Relative Frobenius separation:
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}-I\right\rVert^{2}_{F}>\Delta^{2}\cdot\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}\Sigma_{j}\Sigma_{i}^{-1/2}\right\rVert^{2}
.

In the clustering problem, we are thus given a mixture model where every pair of components is separated in at least one of the three modes of separation above for some
\Delta
depending only on
w_{\min}
.
The key idea in
[
6
]
is to write a sum-of-squares relaxation that attempts to find a cluster that satisfies some concentration (moments of degree
\leq 2
polynomials grow at the expected sub-exponential rate) and anti-concentration (every one-dimensional marginal takes a small value only with a small probability) properties that an actual Gaussian cluster satisfies.
Their analysis shows that these properties characterize actual clusters: any subset of the data that satisfies these properties cannot appreciably intersect two distinct ground truth clusters.
To invoke the above properties, they need efficient algorithms to certify the concentration and anti-concentration properties of the input sample in
all
directions (and degree-
2
polynomials) simultaneously.

Algorithmically verifying the concentration property required to handle relative Frobenius separation needs only
d^{O(1)}
time and samples.
Indeed, this observation is a core component in robustly learning a mixture of
k
arbitrary Gaussians (see Theorem 4.3 in
[
3
]
).

The critical bottleneck in
[
6
]
is the cost of certifying the anti-concentration property, which uses moment-based certificates and necessarily incurs a cost of
d^{\Omega(k)}
in sample complexity, as
\Omega(k)
moments are necessary for their goal. They incur this cost even in the case of centered mixtures, and it is required for their algorithm to cluster mixtures that contain pairs of components that only satisfy condition 2 in
Definition

2.1
.
A concrete example for which the prior methods require
d^{\Omega(k)}
samples is the total-variation separated mixture
\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i}N(0,I-v_{i}v_{i}^{\top})
for arbitrary but distinct unit vectors
v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

2.1
Clustering Mixtures with Centered Components

No parallel pancakes with centered components

Our starting point is the observation that there is no well-separated
1
D mixture with centered components that matches the first
four
moments of a standard Gaussian.
This is in contrast to the parallel pancakes example, which consists of a
1
D mixture of
k
Gaussians that matches the first
\Omega(k)
moments of
N(0,1)
.

Proposition 2.2

(No moment matching with centered mixtures)
.

Let
\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}N(0,\sigma_{i}^{2})
be a mixture of
k
Gaussians that has second moment
1
and fourth moment
3
.
Then
\sigma_{i}^{2}=1
for all
i\in[k]
.

Proof.

Each
N(0,\sigma_{i}^{2})
has second moment
\sigma_{i}^{2}
and fourth moment
3\sigma_{i}^{4}
.
Then
{\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\sigma_{i}^{2}=1}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}(3\sigma_{i}^{4})=3
, so
{\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\sigma_{i}^{4}=1=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\sigma_{i}^{2}\right)^{2}}
, which is only possible if
{\sigma_{1}^{2}=\ldots=\sigma_{k}^{2}=1}
.
∎

Of course, this fact does not necessarily imply efficient clusterability.
In fact, it does not even rule out the existence of two distinct pairs of well-separated centered
1
D mixtures with the same first
\Omega(k)
moments.
Nevertheless, we will crucially use this observation’s high-dimensional variant.

Reduction to tensor subspace recovery

Given the mixture’s fourth moment tensor, we can reduce our task to the following tensor subspace recovery problem.
(For a tensor
T
of order
m
, we define
\operatorname{Sym}(T)=\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\pi}\pi T
over permutations
\pi
of
[m]
, where
(\pi T)_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}}=T_{\pi(i_{1}),\ldots,\pi(i_{m})}
.)

Proposition 2.3

(Tensor subspace recovery, informal)
.

Let
{M_{4}=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\operatorname{Sym}(S_{i}^{\otimes 2})\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{\otimes 4}}}
, where
S_{1},\ldots,S_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
are unknown symmetric matrices.
For each
S_{i}
, define
P_{i}^{\geq\tau}
as the span of the eigenvectors of
S_{i}
with eigenvalues larger than
\tau
in absolute value.
Fix some
\tau>0
, and let
\tau^{\prime}=1/f(k,\tau^{-1})
be small enough.
Then, there exists an algorithm that takes as input
M_{4}
, runs in time
\mathrm{poly}(d)\cdot f(k,\max_{i}\dim(P_{i}^{\geq\tau^{\prime}}))
, and outputs an approximate cover of
P_{1}^{\geq\tau}\cup\ldots\cup P_{k}^{\geq\tau}
.

Let us briefly explain the reduction.
We can assume without loss of generality that the input mixture
\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}N(0,\Sigma_{i})
is in isotropic position
\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\Sigma_{i}=I_{d}
.
Furthermore, if the mixture satisfies
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I_{d}\right\rVert_{F}\gg 1
for any
i
, we can apply an existing algorithm of
[
3
]
to partially cluster the mixture in polynomial time and iterate, so we can assume without loss of generality that
\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-I_{d}\right\rVert_{F}\leq O_{k}(1)
for all
i
.
Under this Frobenius closeness assumption, any two mixture components can only be separated spectrally (Condition 2 in
Definition

2.1
).
Our strategy is then to find a “direction of separation” along which at least two components satisfy spectral separation — by projecting the samples along this direction, we can partially cluster the mixture and iterate.
It turns out that all directions of such separation
v
must satisfy
v^{\top}\Sigma_{i}v\ll 1
for some
i
, so
v
must be close to the eigenspace of
\Sigma_{i}
with eigenvalues bounded away from one.
Hence, applying
Proposition

2.3
with
M_{4}=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\operatorname{Sym}((\Sigma_{i}-I_{d})^{\otimes 2})
(which is the fourth Hermite moment tensor of the mixture and can be estimated from the samples) and
\tau=\Omega_{k}(1)
would yield a cover of all directions of separation.

It remains to argue that applying
Proposition

2.3
takes time
\mathrm{poly}(d)\cdot O_{k}(1)
in our setting.
Since we have that
\left\lVert S_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leq O_{k}(1)
, and because the eigenvectors of
S_{i}
in
P_{i}^{\geq\tau^{\prime}}
have eigenvalues lower bounded by
\tau^{\prime}=\Omega_{k}(1)
in absolute value, we must also have that
\dim(P_{i}^{\geq\tau^{\prime}})\leq O_{k}(1)
for all
i
.
This gives the desired bound on the time complexity.

Let us focus now on the tensor problem in
Proposition

2.3
. For simplicity of notation, we drop the superscript from
P_{1}^{\geq\tau},\ldots,P_{k}^{\geq\tau}
.

Linear algebraic algorithms?

Our algorithm will be based on sum-of-squares relaxations.
One might wonder, however, whether simpler linear algebra techniques might suffice to solve
Proposition

2.3
.
Let us briefly explore this.

To begin with, consider a much simpler setting: Suppose we are given
\widetilde{M}_{4}=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}S_{i}^{\otimes 2}
without the symmetrization operation.
Then, using
\widetilde{M}_{4}
, we can recover the
(\leq k)
-dimensional span of the matrices
\operatorname{span}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{k})
.
Furthermore, because of the bound
\left\lVert S_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leq O_{k}(1)
, we can afford in time
\mathrm{poly}(d)\cdot O_{k}(1)
to enumerate a net of matrices in
\operatorname{span}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{k})
with bounded Frobenius norm such that, for each
S_{i}
, we find some
\hat{S}_{i}
satisfying
\left\lVert S_{i}-\hat{S}_{i}\right\rVert_{F}\leq\Omega_{k}(1)
.
Then the span of the large eigenvectors of
\hat{S}_{i}
must be close to the span of the large eigenvectors of
S_{i}
, and it suffices to include in the output a cover of this span for every matrix that we enumerate.

The symmetrization operation, however, changes the problem significantly, and it is no longer clear how to obtain
\operatorname{span}(S_{1},\ldots,S_{k})
.
This is reminiscent of the prior works on power sum decomposition
[
27
,
26
,
2
]
, where the key idea is also to undo the effect of the symmetrization operation (see also the related approach of
[
13
]
). However, the ideas in those works strongly rely on
\Sigma_{i}
s being
random
(with later works extending this to a setting with smoothing with additive gaussian perturbations) and break down in the setting of this work, where we have no assumptions on
\Sigma_{i}
s except for the total variation separation.

Identifiability via unit vectors

To find a set of interest — in our case,
P_{1}\cup\ldots\cup P_{k}
—, a common approach in the robust statistics literature is to formulate a system of constraints over unit vectors
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that identifies the points in this set.
Then, the hope is that a sum-of-squares relaxation of this program can approximate the solution set.

With this idea in mind, we observe that for a vector
v
we have
\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(v^{\top}S_{i}v)^{2}
.
Hence,
\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle
is large when at least one of
|v^{\top}S_{i}v|
is large.
Let
P_{i}^{+}
and
P_{i}^{-}
be the span of the eigenvectors of
S_{i}
captured by
P_{i}
with positive / negative eigenvalues, such that
P_{i}=\operatorname{span}(P_{i}^{+}\cup P_{i}^{-})
.
Then, for all unit vectors
v
contained in either
P_{i}^{+}
or
P_{i}^{-}
, we have
\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle\geq\tau^{2}/k
.
This observation suggests that, to identify
\operatorname{span}(P_{1}\cup\ldots\cup P_{k})
, it may suffice to identify those unit vectors
v
for which
\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle
is bounded away from zero.
Unfortunately,
\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle
remains large even for
v
that are
\Omega(1)
-far from the span of
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}
, and therefore it describes a set of points whose cover has size exponential in
d
.

Orthogonal complement

Instead, we observe the intriguing fact that
\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle\approx 0
if and only if
v^{\top}S_{i}v\approx 0
simultaneously for all
i
.
This is essentially the opposite of what we are looking for and suggests switching up our goal: what if we search instead for a subspace
orthogonal
to
\operatorname{span}(P_{1}\cup\ldots\cup P_{k})
?
Taking the orthogonal complement of such a subspace would give us
\operatorname{span}(P_{1}\cup\ldots\cup P_{k})
back.

Unfortunately, the condition
\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle\approx 0
does not imply that
v
is orthogonal to
\operatorname{span}(P_{1}\cup\ldots\cup P_{k})
:
v^{\top}S_{i}v
can be
\approx 0
even if
v
is contained in
P_{i}
, if it is split between
P_{i}^{+}
and
P_{i}^{-}
.
The resolution of this issue is a novel condition on
the Hessian
of
\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle
, which allows us to identify only those
v
for which
S_{i}v\approx 0
holds for all
i
.
The condition
S_{i}v\approx 0
is stronger than the condition
v^{\top}S_{i}v\approx 0
, as it implies that
v
is close to the kernel of
S_{i}
, and hence that
v
is nearly orthogonal to
P_{i}
.

Let us illustrate the Hessian condition in the exact case in which
v
lies in the kernel of each
S_{i}
.
For such
v
, a simple calculation shows that
\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle=0
and
\nabla^{2}_{v}\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle=0
.
We prove that the converse is also true:

Proposition 2.4
.

Suppose
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfies
\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle=0
and
\nabla^{2}_{v}\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle=0
.
Then
S_{i}v=0
for all
i
.

Proof.

The Hessian of
\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle
is

\nabla^{2}_{v}\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle=\frac{8}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(S_{i}v)^{\otimes 2}+\frac{4}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(v^{\top}S_{i}v)S_{i}\,.

The assumption
\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle=0
implies that
v^{\top}S_{i}v=0
for all
i
, so we can eliminate the second term.
Hence,

\nabla^{2}_{v}\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle=\frac{8}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(S_{i}v)^{\otimes 2}\,.

Then the assumption
\nabla^{2}\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle=0
implies that
\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(S_{i}v)^{\otimes 2}=0
, which holds only if
S_{i}v=0
for all
i
.
∎

By an approximate version of
Proposition

2.4
, we show that whenever
\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle\approx 0
and
\nabla^{2}_{v}\langle M_{4},v^{\otimes 4}\rangle\approx 0
, we have
\left\lVert P_{i}v\right\rVert\approx 0
for all
i
, where by
P_{i}
we denote here the orthogonal projection to the subspace
P_{i}
.
Furthermore, we can express this proof as a constant-degree sum-of-squares proof.

Sum-of-squares rounding

Hence, we can create a system of polynomial constraints
\mathcal{A}(v)
over a variable
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that implies via constant-degree sum-of-squares proofs that
\left\lVert P_{i}v\right\rVert\approx 0

for all
i
.
We also show that
\mathcal{A}(v)
is non-trivial: there exists a subspace of dimension
d-f(k,\max_{i}\dim(P_{i}^{\geq\tau^{\prime}}))
such that all vectors in it satisfy
\mathcal{A}(v)
(this is a subspace of vectors close to the kernel of each
S_{i}
).
Recall, however, that in the end we are interested in recovering a subspace that includes
P_{1}\cup\ldots\cup P_{k}
, i.e., the opposite of what we have now.

To recover the subspace of interest, we define a new system of constraints
\mathcal{B}(u)
over a variable
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that imposes that
u
is
nearly orthogonal to all
v
that satisfy
\mathcal{A}(v)
.
Then, we show that
\mathcal{B}(u)
implies via low-degree sum-of-squares proofs that
u
is close to an
f(k,\max_{i}\dim(P_{i}^{\geq\tau^{\prime}}))
-dimensional subspace, and furthermore all
u
with
\left\lVert P_{i}u\right\rVert\approx 1
for at least one
P_{i}
are feasible for
\mathcal{B}(u)
.

Finally, we design and analyze a rounding algorithm for
\mathcal{B}(u)
that recovers a subspace of dimension
f(k,\max_{i}\dim(P_{i}^{\geq\tau^{\prime}}))
, which approximately contains all the vectors
u
feasible for
\mathcal{B}(u)
.
Hence, this subspace approximately contains
P_{1}\cup\ldots\cup P_{k}
, solving the tensor problem.
Crucially, because all the sum-of-squares proofs involved have constant degree, the time complexity of the algorithm is polynomial in
d
.

2.2
Clustering Mixtures with Identical-Covariance Components

We explain now how these ideas also apply to the case of identical-covariance mixtures.
Consider a distribution
\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}N(\mu_{i},\Sigma)
with components separated in total variation distance.
We can assume without loss of generality isotropic position
\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}+\Sigma=I_{d}
.

Our strategy, as in the centered case, is to find a direction of separation along which to partially cluster the mixture and then iterate.
A simple argument shows that all directions of separation
v
must satisfy
v^{\top}\Sigma v\ll 1
, so
v
must be close to the eigenspace of
\Sigma
with eigenvalues close to zero.
Furthermore, by the isotropic position,
\Sigma
has at least
d-k
eigenvalues equal to
1
.
Therefore, all directions of separation are close to a subspace of dimension
k
.

Identifiability via
O(\log k)
moments

Unfortunately, unlike the case of mixtures with centered components, for mixtures with identical-covariance components the fourth moment tensor may exactly match that of
N(0,I_{d})
, giving no information about the components.
We rely instead on a result of Buhai and Steurer
[
12
]
, who proved that an identical-covariance mixture of Gaussians cannot match the first
O(\log k)
moments of
N(0,I_{d})
.
By an adjustment of their argument, we show that if the mixture matches the first
O(\log k)
moments of
N(0,1)
when projected onto a direction
v
, then
v
must be orthogonal to all
\mu_{i}
s, and hence
v^{\top}\Sigma v=1
.

Then, we consider the system of polynomial constraints over a variable
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that imposes that the first
O(\log k)
moments of the mixture projected in direction
v
are approximately equal to those of
N(0,1)
.
We can prove that this system implies, via
O(\log k)
-degree sum-of-squares proofs, that
v^{\top}\Sigma v\approx 1
.

Sum-of-squares rounding

As in the case of zero-mean components, we ended up with a system of constraints that identifies the opposite of what we want: in the end, we are interested in the eigenspace of
\Sigma
with eigenvalues close to zero.
Hence, we apply the same strategy as before: we create a new system of constraints over a variable
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
which imposes that
u
is nearly orthogonal to all
v
that satisfy our system of constraints.
As before, we can round this system of constraints to obtain a subspace of dimension
O_{k}(1)
that approximately contains the eigenspace of
\Sigma
with eigenvalues close to zero.
Because the sum-of-squares proofs involved here have degree
O(\log k)
, the time complexity of this algorithm ends up including a term
d^{\mathrm{polylog}(k)}
.

3
Preliminaries

While we will omit discussions of the computational model and numerical precision in this work, we note that our algorithms can be implemented to give guarantees in the standard bit complexity model.
For a detailed discussion, we direct the reader to Section 3.1 of
[
32
]
.

3.1
Sum-of-Squares Proofs

In this section we give a brief introduction to sum-of-square proofs, taken from the corresponding section in
[
12
]
.
For a more detailed exposition, see the lecture notes
[
8
]
or the monograph
[
25
]
.

Definition 3.1

(Sum-of-squares proofs)
.

Let
p(x)
and
q_{1}(x),...,q_{m}(x)
be polynomials over
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and let
\mathcal{A}=\{q_{1}(x)\geq 0,...,q_{m}(x)\geq 0\}
be a system of polynomial inequalities.
A sum-of-squares proof of degree
t
that
p(x)\geq 0
under
\mathcal{A}
is an identity of the form

p(x)=\sum_{S\subseteq[m]}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m_{S}}r_{S,i}(x)^{2}\right)\prod_{j\in S}q_{j}(x)

for polynomials
r_{S,i}(x)
, such that
\max_{S,i}\operatorname{deg}(r_{S,i}(x)^{2}\prod_{j\in S}q_{j}(x))\leq t
.

If there exists a sum-of-squares proof of degree
t
that
p(x)\geq 0
under
\mathcal{A}
, we write
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geq 0
.
We also use the notation
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geq q(x)
if
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)-q(x)\geq 0
and
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\leq q(x)
if
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}q(x)-p(x)\geq 0
.
If
\mathcal{A}=\emptyset
, we omit it altogether and write
\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geq 0
.
We also sometimes omit
\mathcal{A}
if it is clear from context what axioms are assumed.
We note that if
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geq q(x)
and
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{7.77426pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-7.77426pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[7.77426pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-7.77426pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[7.77426pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{s}$}}}$}}}q(x)\geq r(x)
, then
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geq r(x)
, which allows writing chains of inequalities of the form
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geq s(x)\geq r(x)
.

Pseudo-distributions and pseudo-expectations

Definition 3.2

(Pseudo-distributions)
.

A pseudo-distribution
D
of degree
t
is a function from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}
with finite support such that
\sum_{x\in\operatorname{supp}(D)}D(x)=1
and
\sum_{x\in\operatorname{supp}(D)}D(x)p(x)^{2}\geq 0
for all polynomials
p(x)
with
\operatorname{deg}(p(x)^{2})\leq t
.

Definition 3.3

(Pseudo-expectations)
.

Given a pseudo-distribution
D
of degree
t
, the associated pseudo-expectation
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{D(x)}
is defined by
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{D(x)}f(x)=\sum_{x\in\operatorname{supp}(D)}D(x)f(x)
for a function
f(x)
.

We now define the notion of a pseudo-distribution that satisfies a set of polynomial inequalities.

Definition 3.4

(Constrained pseudo-distributions)
.

A pseudo-distribution
D
of degree
t
satisfies the set of polynomial inequalities
\mathcal{A}=\{q_{1}(x)\geq 0,...,q_{m}(x)\geq 0\}
if, for all
S\subseteq[m]
,
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{D(x)}r(x)^{2}\prod_{j\in S}q_{j}(x)\geq 0
for all polynomials
r(x)
such that
\operatorname{deg}(r(x)^{2}\prod_{j\in S}q_{j}(x))\leq t
.

D
approximately satisfies
\mathcal{A}
up to error
\eta
if, under the same conditions as in the previous case,
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{D(x)}r(x)^{2}\prod_{j\in S}q_{j}(x)\geq-\eta\|r(x)^{2}\|\prod_{j\in S}\|q_{j}(x)\|
, where
\|p(x)\|
denotes the
2
-norm of the vector of coefficients of the polynomial
p(x)
.

The connection between pseudo-distributions and sum-of-squares proofs is made in
Fact

3.5
, which shows that if a pseudo-distribution satisfies a set of polynomial inequalities, then it also satisfies any other polynomial inequalities derived from this set through sum-of-squares proofs.

Fact 3.5
.

If
D
is a pseudo-distribution of degree
t
that satisfies
\mathcal{A}
and if
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{s}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}p(x)\geq 0
, then
{\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{D(x)}r(x)^{2}p(x)\geq 0}
for all polynomials
r(x)
such that
\operatorname{deg}(r(x)^{2}p(x))\leq t
.
If
D
approximately satisfies
\mathcal{A}
up to error
\eta
, then, under the same conditions as in the previous case,

{\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{D(x)}r(x)^{2}p(x)\geq-\eta\|r(x)^{2}\|\|p(x)\|}\,.

Finally,
Fact

3.6
shows that there exists an algorithm with time complexity
(n+m)^{O(t)}
to compute a pseudo-distribution of degree
t
that approximately satisfies
\mathcal{A}
up to error
2^{-n^{\Theta(t)}}
.

Fact 3.6
.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, if
\mathcal{A}=\{q_{1}(x)\geq 0,...,q_{m}(x)\geq 0\}
is feasible and explicitly bounded,
9
9

9

Explicit boundedness means that
\mathcal{A}
contains a constraint of the form
x_{1}^{2}+...+x_{n}^{2}\leq B
.
In our applications it is possible to add such a constraint with
B
large enough such that the constraint is always satisfied by the intended solution.

then there exists an algorithm that runs in time
(n+m)^{O(t)}
and computes a pseudo-distribution of degree
t
that approximately satisfies
\mathcal{A}
up to error
2^{-n^{\Theta(t)}}
.
10
10

10

In our applications this error is negligible.

Common sum-of-squares proofs

We state now some widely used sum-of-squares proofs that we use in the current paper.

Fact 3.7

(Cauchy-Schwarz, see Lemma A.1 in
[
41
]
)
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be vectors of indeterminates. Then

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}$}}}\left\{\langle x,y\rangle^{2}\leq\lVert x\rVert^{2}\lVert y\rVert^{2}\right\}\,.

Furthermore, if
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a constant vector, then

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\left\{\langle x,y\rangle^{2}\leq\lVert x\rVert^{2}\lVert y\rVert^{2}\right\}\,.

Fact 3.8

(Almost triangle inequality, see fact A.6 in
[
30
]
)
.

Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\in\mathbb{R}
be indeterminates. Then

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{30.78017pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-30.78017pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[30.78017pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-30.78017pt\raisebox{3.36111pt}{\makebox[30.78017pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x_{1},\dots,x_{r}}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}\right)^{2t}\leq r^{2t-1}\sum_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}^{2t}\right\}\,.

Fact 3.9

(AM-GM, see Lemma A.1 in
[
7
]
)
.

Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\in\mathbb{R}
be indeterminates. Then

\left\{x_{1}\geq 0,\ldots,x_{r}\geq 0\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{33.03017pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-33.03017pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[33.03017pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{r}$}}}\hskip-33.03017pt\raisebox{3.96112pt}{\makebox[33.03017pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}}$}}}$}}}\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}\leq\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}x_{i}^{r}\right\}\,.

Fact 3.10

(Square)
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
be indeterminates. Then

\left\{x+y\geq 0,x\leq y\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\left\{x^{2}\leq y^{2}\right\}\,.

Proof.

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}$}}}y^{2}-x^{2}=(y-x)(x+y)\geq 0\,.

∎

Fact 3.11

(Root, see Lemma A.3 in
[
36
]
)
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}
be an indeterminate and let
C\geq 0
be a constant. Then

\left\{x^{2t}\leq C^{2t}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{11.00691pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-11.00691pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[11.00691pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\left\{x\leq C\right\}\,.

Fact 3.12

(Simplification, see Lemma 9.3 in
[
6
]
)
.

Let
x,C\in\mathbb{R}
be indeterminates. Then

\left\{x\geq 0,x^{t}\leq Cx^{t-1}\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{17.02455pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-17.02455pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[17.02455pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2t}$}}}\hskip-17.02455pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[17.02455pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,C}$}}}$}}}\left\{x^{2t}\leq C^{2t}\right\}\,.

Fact 3.13

(Spectral norm bound)
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a vector of indeterminates and let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a constant symmetric matrix. Then

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\left\{x^{\top}Ax\leq\lVert A\rVert\lVert x\rVert^{2}\right\}\,.

Proof.

Let the eigendecomposition of
A
be
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}
with
|\lambda_{i}|\leq\lVert A\rVert
for all
i
. Then

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.53467pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2}$}}}\hskip-8.53467pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.53467pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}x^{\top}Ax=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\langle v_{i},x\rangle^{2}\leq\lVert A\rVert\sum_{i}\langle v_{i},x\rangle^{2}=\lVert A\rVert\lVert x\rVert^{2}\,.

∎

Fact 3.14

(Bilinear form bound)
.

Let
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be vectors of indeterminates and let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a constant symmetric matrix. Then

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}$}}}\left\{(x^{\top}Ay)^{2}\leq\lVert A\rVert^{2}\lVert x\rVert^{2}\lVert y\rVert^{2}\right\}\,.

Proof.

We have by
Fact

3.7
and
Fact

3.13
that

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{15.10532pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{4}$}}}\hskip-15.10532pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[15.10532pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x,y}$}}}$}}}(x^{\top}Ay)^{2}\leq\lVert x\rVert^{2}\lVert Ay\rVert^{2}\leq\lVert A\rVert^{2}\lVert x\rVert^{2}\lVert y\rVert^{2}\,.

∎

Fact 3.15

(Expectation Cauchy-Schwarz)
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a vector of indeterminates and let
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a random variable supported on
{\mathcal{A}}
such that all its moments are finite.
Let
p_{a}(x)=p(x,a)
and
q_{a}(x)=q(x,a)
be polynomials in
x
and
a
that have degrees
in
x
at most
r
and
s
, respectively. Then we have

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{28.07759pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-28.07759pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[28.07759pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2(r+s)}$}}}\hskip-28.07759pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[28.07759pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}p_{\bm{a}}(x)q_{\bm{a}}(x)\right)^{2}\leq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}p_{\bm{a}}(x)^{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}q_{\bm{a}}(x)^{2}\right\}\,.

Proof.

Let
\mu
be the probability measure associated with
\bm{a}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{28.07759pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-28.07759pt\raisebox{-7.21112pt}{\makebox[28.07759pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2(r+s)}$}}}\hskip-28.07759pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[28.07759pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}

\displaystyle 2\mathop{\mathbb{E}}p_{\bm{a}}(x)^{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}q_{\bm{a}}(x)^{2}-2\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}p_{\bm{a}}(x)q_{\bm{a}}(x)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=2\left(\int_{a}p_{a}(x)^{2}d\mu(a)\right)\left(\int_{a}q_{a}(x)^{2}d\mu(a)\right)-2\left(\int_{a}p_{a}(x)q_{a}(x)d\mu(a)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{a}\int_{a^{\prime}}\left(p_{a}(x)^{2}q_{a^{\prime}}(x)^{2}+p_{a^{\prime}}(x)^{2}q_{a}(x)^{2}-2p_{a}(x)q_{a}(x)p_{a^{\prime}}(x)q_{a^{\prime}}(x)\right)d\mu(a)d\mu(a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{a}\int_{a^{\prime}}\left(p_{a}(x)q_{a^{\prime}}(x)-p_{a^{\prime}}(x)q_{a}(x)\right)^{2}d\mu(a)d\mu(a^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq 0\,.

To explain the result more explicitly, let
z_{a,a^{\prime}}(x)=\left(p_{a}(x)q_{a^{\prime}}(x)-p_{a^{\prime}}(x)q_{a}(x)\right)^{2}
. We argued that
z_{a,a^{\prime}}(x)
is the square of a polynomial in
x
of degree at most
2(r+s)
.
Therefore, for each
a,a^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}
we can write
z_{a,a^{\prime}}(x)=\langle M_{a,a^{\prime}},(1,x)^{\otimes 2(r+s)}\rangle

for some positive semidefinite matrix
M_{a,a^{\prime}}
whose entries are polynomials in
a,a^{\prime}
. Then

\int_{a,a^{\prime}}\left\langle M_{a,a^{\prime}},(1,x)^{\otimes 2(r+s)}\right\rangle d\mu(a)d\mu(a^{\prime})=\left\langle\int_{a,a^{\prime}}M_{a,a^{\prime}}d\mu(a)d\mu(a^{\prime}),(1,x)^{\otimes 2(r+s)}\right\rangle\,,

which is a sum of squares because
\int_{a,a^{\prime}}M_{a,a^{\prime}}d\mu(a)d\mu(a^{\prime})
is also a positive semidefinite matrix.
∎

Fact 3.16

(Jensen’s inequality for even powers)
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a vector of indeterminates and let
\bm{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a random variable supported on
{\mathcal{A}}
such that all its moments are finite. Let
p_{a}(x)=p(x,a)
be a polynomial in
x
and
a
that has degree
in
x
at most
r
. Then we have

\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{14.93517pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-14.93517pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[14.93517pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2tr}$}}}\hskip-14.93517pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[14.93517pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{x}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}p_{\bm{a}}(x)^{2}\right)^{t}\leq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}p_{\bm{a}}(x)^{2t}\right\}\,.

Proof.

Let
\mu
be the probability measure associated with
\bm{a}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{14.93517pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-14.93517pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[14.93517pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2tr}$}}}\hskip-14.93517pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[14.93517pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}p_{\bm{a}}(x)^{2t}-\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}p_{\bm{a}}(x)^{2}\right)^{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{a}p_{a}(x)^{2t}d\mu(a)\right)-\left(\int_{a}p_{a}(x)^{2}d\mu(a)\right)^{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}}\left(\frac{1}{t}\left(p_{a_{1}}(x)^{2t}+\ldots+p_{a_{t}}(x)^{2t}\right)-p_{a_{1}}(x)^{2}\cdots p_{a_{t}}(x)^{2}\right)d\mu(a_{1})\cdots d\mu(a_{t})\,,

where for each term in the integral we have by
Fact

3.9

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{14.93517pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-14.93517pt\raisebox{-6.47223pt}{\makebox[14.93517pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{2tr}$}}}\hskip-14.93517pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[14.93517pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\frac{1}{t}\left(p_{a_{1}}(x)^{2t}+\ldots+p_{a_{t}}(x)^{2t}\right)-p_{a_{1}}(x)^{2}\cdots p_{a_{t}}(x)^{2}\geq 0\,.

To explain the result more explicitly, let

z_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}}(x)=\frac{1}{t}\left(p_{a_{1}}(x)^{2t}+\ldots+p_{a_{t}}(x)^{2t}\right)-p_{a_{1}}(x)^{2}\cdots p_{a_{t}}(x)^{2}\,.

We argued that
z_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}}(x)
is a sum of squares of polynomials in
x
each of degree at most
2tr
. Therefore, for each
a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}\in{\mathcal{A}}
we can write
z_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}}(x)=\langle M_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}},(1,x)^{\otimes 2tr}\rangle

for some positive semidefinite matrix
M_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}}
whose entries are polynomials in
a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}
. Then

\int_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}}\left\langle M_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}},(1,x)^{\otimes 2tr}\right\rangle d\mu(a_{1})\cdots d\mu(a_{t})=\left\langle\int_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}}M_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}}d\mu(a_{1})\cdots d\mu(a_{t}),(1,x)^{\otimes 2tr}\right\rangle\,,

which is a sum of squares because
\int_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}}M_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{t}}d\mu(a_{1})\cdots d\mu(a_{t})
is also a positive semidefinite matrix.
∎

3.2
Gaussian Distributions

In this section, we restate some existing results about Gaussians and mixtures of Gaussians that we use in our analysis.

First, we state a relation between total variation distance and parameter distance developed in
[
6
]
:

Fact 3.17

(Gaussian TV distance and parameters, Proposition A.1 in
[
6
]
)
.

Fix
\Delta>0
and let
\mu,\mu^{\prime}
and
\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}\succ 0
satisfy:

1.

Mean closeness:
\forall v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\langle\mu-\mu^{\prime},v\rangle^{2}\leq\Delta^{2}\cdot v^{\top}(\Sigma+\Sigma^{\prime})v
,

2.

Spectral closeness:
\forall v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\frac{1}{\Delta^{2}}v^{\top}\Sigma v\leq v^{\top}\Sigma^{\prime}v\leq\Delta^{2}\cdot v^{\top}\Sigma v
,

3.

Relative Frobenius closeness:
\left\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}\Sigma^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1/2}-I\right\rVert_{F}^{2}\leq\Delta^{2}\cdot\left\lVert\Sigma^{-1/2}\Sigma^{\prime}\Sigma^{-1/2}\right\rVert^{2}
.

Then
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(N(\mu,\Sigma),N(\mu^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime}))\leq 1-\exp(-O(\Delta^{2}\log\Delta))
.

Second, we state the guarantees of an algorithm of
[
17
]
for robustly learning a Gaussian:

Fact 3.18

(Robust Gaussian learning, Theorem 1.2 in
[
17
]
)
.

Let
\mu,\Sigma
be arbitrary and unknown, and let
\varepsilon,\tau>0
.
There is a polynomial time algorithm which given
\varepsilon
,
\tau
, and an
\varepsilon
-corrupted
set of
n
samples from
N(\mu,\Sigma)
with
n\geq\tilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{d^{2}\log^{5}(1/\tau)}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
, produces
\hat{\mu}
and
\hat{\Sigma}
such that with probability
1-\tau
we have

d_{\mathrm{TV}}\left(N(\mu,\Sigma),N(\hat{\mu},\hat{\Sigma}\right)\leq O(\varepsilon\log^{3/2}(1/\varepsilon))\,.

For mixtures of Gaussians, we start by stating the identifiability and recovery results in the state-of-the-art paper of
[
3
]
:

Fact 3.19

(Gaussian mixture identifiability, Theorem 9.1 in
[
3
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}}w_{i}G_{i}
,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{2}}w_{i}^{\prime}G_{i}^{\prime}
be two mixtures of Gaussians such that
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\leq\varepsilon
.
Then there exists a partition of
[k_{1}]
into sets
R_{0},R_{1},\ldots,R_{\ell}
and a partition of
[k_{2}]
into sets
S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{\ell}
such that

1.

Let
W_{i}=\sum_{j\in R_{i}}w_{j}
for
i=0,1,\ldots,k_{1}
,
W_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\in S_{i}}w_{j}^{\prime}
for
i=0,1,\ldots,k_{2}
. Then for all
i\in[\ell]
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert W_{i}-W_{i}^{\prime}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{poly}_{k}(\varepsilon)\,,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{TV}}(G_{j},G_{j^{\prime}}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{poly}_{k}(\varepsilon)\,,\;\;\forall j\in R_{i},j^{\prime}\in S_{i}\,,

2.

W_{0},W_{0}^{\prime}\leq\mathrm{poly}_{k}(\varepsilon)
.

Theorem 3.20

(Gaussian mixture recovery, Theorem 1.6 in
[
3
]
)
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
and a multiset of
n=d^{O(k)}\mathrm{poly}_{k}(1/\varepsilon)
samples from a distribution
F
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(F,\mathcal{M})\leq\varepsilon
, for an unknown target
k
-GMM
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}),
the algorithm runs in time
\mathrm{poly}(n)\mathrm{poly}_{k}(1/\varepsilon)
and outputs a
k^{\prime}
-GMM hypothesis
\hat{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k^{\prime}}\hat{w_{i}}\mathcal{N}(\hat{\mu_{i}},\hat{\Sigma_{i}})
, with
k^{\prime}\leq k
such that with high probability we have that there exists a partition of
[k]
into
k^{\prime}+1
sets
R_{0},R_{1},\ldots,R_{k^{\prime}}
such that

1.

Let
W_{i}=\sum_{j\in R_{i}}w_{j}
,
i\in\{0,1,\ldots,k^{\prime}\}.
Then, for all
i\in[k^{\prime}]
, we have that

\displaystyle\left\lvert W_{i}-\hat{w_{i}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{poly}_{k}(\varepsilon)\,,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j},\Sigma_{j}),\mathcal{N}(\hat{\mu_{i}},\hat{\Sigma_{i}}))

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{poly}_{k}(\varepsilon)\,,\;\;\forall j\in R_{i}\,.

2.

The total weight of exceptional components in
R_{0}
is
W_{0}\leq\mathrm{poly}_{k}(\varepsilon)
.

The same paper also gives a polynomial-time algorithm for putting a mixture of Gaussians in the isotropic position:

Fact 3.21

(Gaussian mixture robust isotropic position, implied by Lemma 6.7 in
[
3
]
)
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
,
11
11

11

The result assumes that
\varepsilon>0
is dimension-independent.

k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\alpha=\varepsilon^{1/(10C^{k+1}(k+1)!)}
.
Let
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}N(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
be a mixture with
w_{i}\geq\alpha
for all
i\in[k]
.
Let
\mu
and
\Sigma
be the mean and covariance of
\mathcal{M}
such that
r=\mathrm{rank}(\Sigma)
and for all
i,j\in[k]
,
\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j})\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}\leq 1/\sqrt{\alpha}
.
Let
X
be a set of points from
\mathcal{M}
.
Given a set
Y
, an
\varepsilon
-corrupted version of
X
, of size
n\geq n_{0}=d^{O(1)}
, there exists an algorithm that takes
Y
as input and in time
n^{O(1)}
outputs with high probability estimators
\hat{\mu}
and
\hat{\Sigma}
such that
\hat{\Sigma}=\hat{U}\hat{\Lambda}\hat{U}^{\top}
is the eigenvalue decomposition, where
\hat{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times r}
has orthonormal columns and
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
is a diagonal matrix.
Further, we can obtain
n
samples
Y^{\prime}
by applying the affine transformation
y_{i}\to\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}(y_{i}-\hat{\mu})
to each sample, such that a
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction have mean
\mu^{\prime}
and covariance
\Sigma^{\prime}
satisfying

1.

\left\lVert\mu^{\prime}\right\rVert\leq O\left(\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}k}{\alpha}\right)\sqrt{\varepsilon/\alpha}\right)
,

2.

\left(\frac{1}{1+(k\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha)}\right)I_{r}\preceq\Sigma^{\prime}\preceq\left(\frac{1}{1-(k\sqrt{\varepsilon}/\alpha)}\right)I_{r}
,

3.

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\prime}-I_{r}\right\rVert_{F}\leq O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}k/\alpha)
,

where
I_{r}
is the
r
-dimensional identity matrix, and the remaining points are arbitrary.
Let
X^{\prime}
be the set obtained by
\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}(x_{i}-\hat{\mu})
.
Then, the points in
X^{\prime}
are distributed as a set of i.i.d. points from the mixture
\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}N(\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}(\mu_{i}-\hat{\mu}),\hat{U}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\Sigma_{i}\hat{\Sigma}^{\dagger/2}\hat{U})
, and
Y^{\prime}
is an
\varepsilon
-corruption of
X^{\prime}
.

Finally,
[
3
]
also give a polynomial-time algorithm for partially clustering a mixture of Gaussians in which two components have Frobenius separation:

Fact 3.22

(Gaussian mixture robust Frobenius clustering, Theorem 4.3 in
[
3
]
)
.

Let
0\leq\varepsilon<\alpha/k\leq 1
and
t\in\mathbb{N}
.
There is an algorithm with the following guarantees: Let
\{\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}\}_{i\leq k}
be means and covariances of
k
unknown Gaussians.
Let
Y
be an
\varepsilon
-corruption of a sample
X
of size
n\geq\mathrm{poly}(d^{t},k^{k+t},\varepsilon^{-1})
from
\mathcal{M}=\sum_{i}w_{i}N(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
.
Suppose further that
w_{i}\geq\alpha>2\varepsilon
for each
i\in[k]
, and that for some
t\in\mathbb{N},\beta>0
there exist
i,j\leq k
such that
\left\lVert\Sigma^{\dagger/2}(\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma_{j})\Sigma^{\dagger/2}\right\rVert_{F}^{2}=\Omega\left((k^{2}t^{4})/(\beta^{2/t}\alpha^{4})\right)
, where
\Sigma
is the covariance of the mixture
\mathcal{M}
.
Then, the algorithm runs in time
n^{O(t)}
, and with high probability over the input, and with probability at least
2^{-O\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{k}{\beta}\right)\right)}
over the random choices of the algorithm, outputs a partition
Y=Y_{1}\cup Y_{2}
satisfying:

1.

Partition respects clustering: for each
i
,

\max\left\{\frac{1}{w_{i}n}\left\lvert Y_{1}\cap X_{i}\right\rvert,\frac{1}{w_{i}n}\left\lvert Y_{2}\cap X_{i}\right\rvert\right\}\geq 1-\beta-O(\varepsilon/\alpha^{4})\,,

2.

Partition is non-trivial:

\max_{i}\frac{1}{w_{i}n}\left\lvert X_{i}\cap Y_{1}\right\rvert,\max_{i}\frac{1}{w_{i}n}\left\lvert X_{i}\cap Y_{2}\right\rvert\geq 1-\beta-O(\varepsilon/\alpha^{4})\,.

3.3
Hypothesis Selection

Our algorithm will produce a large list of candidate mixtures, such that at least one of them is close to the ground truth.
The following hypothesis selection result allows us to select a good mixture from this list of candidate mixtures:

Fact 3.23

(Robust tournament, Lemma 2.9 in
[
34
]
)
.

Let
X
be an unknown distribution and let
H_{1},\ldots,H_{n}
be distributions with explicitly computable probability density functions that can be efficiently sampled from.
Assume furthermore that
\min_{1\leq i\leq n}(d_{\mathrm{TV}}(X,H_{i}))\leq\varepsilon
.
Then there exists an algorithm that given access to
\mathrm{poly}(n,\varepsilon^{-1})

\varepsilon
-noisy samples from
X
along with
H_{1},\ldots,H_{n}
computes in time
\mathrm{poly}(n,\varepsilon^{-1})
a
1\leq m\leq n
so that with high probability

d_{\mathrm{TV}}(X,H_{m})=O(\varepsilon)\,.

3.4
Partial Clustering Definitions

In this section we introduce some definitions and notation relating to clustering that we use in our analysis.

Definition 3.24

(Containment)
.

For a collection of samples
\mathcal{T}
coming from a mixture of components and for a subset
S\subseteq\mathcal{T}
, a component
i
is contained in
S
if a
(1-w_{\min})
-fraction of the samples in
\mathcal{T}
from component
i
are in
S
.
Let
\mathsf{comp}(S)
denote the indices of components contained in
S
.

Definition 3.25

(Corruptions)
.

For a collection of samples
\mathcal{T}
coming from a mixture of components and for a subset
S\subseteq\mathcal{T}
, let
\mathsf{corr}(S)
denote the number of corrupted samples in
S
, defined as the number of samples in
S
that do not belong to a component in
\mathsf{comp}(S)
plus the number of samples in
\mathcal{T}\setminus S
that belong to a component in
\mathsf{comp}(S)
.

Definition 3.26

(
\varepsilon
-good partial clustering)
.

For a collection of samples
\mathcal{T}
coming from a mixture of components, we call a set
\mathcal{S}=\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{k^{\prime}}\}
with
S_{1},\ldots,S_{k^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
a partial clustering of the samples if
S_{1},...,S_{k^{\prime}}
are disjoint,
S_{1}\cup\ldots\cup S_{k^{\prime}}=\mathcal{T}
, and
\mathsf{comp}(S_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{comp}(S_{k^{\prime}})>0
.
We say that a partial clustering
\mathcal{S}
is
(1-\varepsilon)
-good if all
S\in\mathcal{S}
have
\mathsf{corr}(S)/|S|\leq\varepsilon
.

Definition 3.27

(Refinement of a partial clustering)
.

For a collection of samples
\mathcal{T}
coming from a mixture of components, given two partial clusterings of the samples
\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, we say that
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is a refinement of
\mathcal{S}
if for all
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
there exists some
S\in\mathcal{S}
such that
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
and if
\left\lvert\mathcal{S^{\prime}}\right\rvert>\left\lvert\mathcal{S}\right\rvert
.

4
Efficiently Identifying Relevant Subspaces via SoS

In this section we present two general tools for identifying “relevant” low-dimensional subspaces given input data.
These results form the technical core of our dimension reduction subroutine.

For the first result, suppose we have a system of polynomial constraints
{\mathcal{A}}
(built from the input data) that implies that all solutions to
{\mathcal{A}}
are (approximately) contained in a low-dimensional subspace.
Then, if this implication holds in the low-degree sum-of-squares proof system, we show that there exists an efficient algorithm that recovers a low-dimensional subspace that (approximately) contains all solutions to
{\mathcal{A}}
.

Theorem 4.1

(Identifying subspace approximately containing all solutions)
.

There is an algorithm that takes input a system of polynomial inequalities
\mathcal{A}=\{\lVert v\rVert^{2}=1,p_{1}(v)\geq 0,\ldots,p_{m}(v)\geq 0\}
in indeterminate
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and outputs an orthogonal projection matrix
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with the following guarantee:

Suppose there exists an orthogonal projection matrix
P\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
of rank
r
such that
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\{\|Pv\|^{2}\geq 1-\varepsilon\}
for some
\varepsilon<1
small enough.
Then, for
\gamma\geq 2\sqrt{2}\varepsilon^{1/8}
, the algorithm runs in time
(dm)^{O(t)}\cdot O(1/\gamma)^{r}
and outputs an orthogonal projection matrix
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
\mathrm{rank}(Q)\leq O(1/\gamma)^{r}
such that with high probability over the random choices of the algorithm, for every unit vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying
{\mathcal{A}}
, there exists a unit vector
v^{\prime}
such that
Qv^{\prime}=v^{\prime}
and
\left\lVert v-v^{\prime}\right\rVert\leq\gamma
.

For the second result, suppose instead that
{\mathcal{A}}
implies that all solutions to
{\mathcal{A}}
are (approximately)
orthogonal
to a subspace of interest.
If, in addition, we have the converse fact that there exists a low-dimensional subspace such that all vectors orthogonal to it are solutions to
{\mathcal{A}}
, then we give an efficient algorithm that recovers a low-dimensional subspace that (approximately) contains the original subspace of interest.

In our proofs we actually need a slightly more general form of this result, in which solutions to
{\mathcal{A}}
are (approximately) orthogonal to
multiple
low-dimensional subspaces.
We state this more general version.

Theorem 4.2

(Identifying subspace approximately orthogonal to all solutions)
.

There is an algorithm that takes input a system of polynomial inequalities
\mathcal{A}=\{\lVert v\rVert^{2}\leq 1,p_{1}(v)\geq 0,\ldots,p_{m}(v)\geq 0\}
in indeterminate
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and outputs an orthogonal projection matrix
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with the following guarantee:

Suppose there exist orthogonal projection matrices
P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that
{\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\{\|P_{i}v\|^{2}\leq\varepsilon\}}
for all
i\in[k]
and for some
\varepsilon<1
small enough.
Suppose further that there exists an orthogonal projection matrix
R\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
of rank
r
such that

\left\{\lVert v\rVert^{2}\leq 1,Rv=0\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}{\mathcal{A}}\,.

Then, for
\gamma\geq 4\varepsilon^{1/8}
, the algorithm runs in time
(dm)^{O(t^{3})}\cdot O(1/\gamma)^{r}
and outputs an orthogonal projection matrix
Q\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
\mathrm{rank}(Q)\leq O(1/\gamma)^{r}
such that with high probability over the random choices of the algorithm, for every unit vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\|P_{i}v\|^{2}\geq 1-\varepsilon
for some
i\in[k]
, there exists a unit vector
v^{\prime}
such that
Qv^{\prime}=v^{\prime}
and
\left\lVert v-v^{\prime}\right\rVert\leq\gamma
.

Furthermore, if in each sum-of-squares proof
{\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\{\|P_{i}v\|^{2}\leq\varepsilon\}}
each monomial
r(v)^{2}\prod_{i\in S}p_{i}(v)
has
|S|\leq q
, then the time complexity is
(dm)^{O(t^{2}(q+1))}\cdot O(1/\gamma)^{r}
.

4.1
Proof of
Theorem

4.1

We prove
Theorem

4.1
by analyzing the following algorithm:

Algorithm 1

Subspace rounding

1

Q=0^{d\times d}
;

2

for

i=1
to
O(1/\gamma)^{r}

do

3

if

\exists
degree-
t
pseudo-expectation
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
that satisfies
\mathcal{A}\cup\{\lVert Qv\rVert^{2}\leq 1-\gamma^{2}/2\}

then

4

Let
M=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}vv^{\top}
;

5

Fix an eigendecomposition of
M
, and let
\mathcal{D}_{M}
be the distribution over the eigenvectors in the decomposition such that the probability of each eigenvector is proportional to its corresponding eigenvalue;

6

Sample
w
from
\mathcal{D}_{M}
conditioned on
\lVert Qw\rVert^{2}\leq 1-\gamma^{2}/4
;

7

Update
Q
to be the orthogonal projection to the subspace spanned by
Q
and
w
;

8

end if

9

else

10

return

Q
;

11

end if

12

end for

Proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

First note that
\mathrm{rank}(Q)\leq O(1/\gamma)^{r}
, because at each iteration of the for loop we only increase the dimension of the subspace associated with
Q
by
1
.

Next, we prove that as long as the algorithm finds a degree-
t
pseudo-expectation
\tilde{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}
that satisfies
\mathcal{A}\cup\{\lVert Qv\rVert^{2}\leq 1-\gamma^{2}/2\}
, it samples with probability
1-\varepsilon^{1/4}
some
w
such that
\lVert Pw\rVert^{2}\geq 1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}
and
\lVert Qw\rVert^{2}\leq 1-\gamma^{2}/4
.
Because
\tilde{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}
is a pseudo-expectation of degree at least
2
, we have that
M\succeq 0
.
Also, because
{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\lVert v\rVert^{2}=1
, we have that
\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(M)=1
.
Fix an eigenvalue decomposition of
M
as
M=\lambda_{1}z_{1}z_{1}^{\top}+\ldots+\lambda_{d}z_{d}z_{d}^{\top}
where
\lVert z_{i}\rVert=1
for all
i
.
Then
\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(M)=1
implies that
\lambda_{1}+...+\lambda_{d}=1
.

Because
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.25113pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{-6.26668pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\hskip-8.25113pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.25113pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\|Pv\|^{2}\geq 1-\varepsilon
, we have that
\langle P,M\rangle\geq 1-\varepsilon
, so
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}\langle P,z_{i}z_{i}^{\top}\rangle\geq 1-\varepsilon
.
Let
S=\{i\mid\langle P,z_{i}z_{i}^{\top}\rangle\geq 1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\}
.
Then we must have
\sum_{i\in S}\lambda_{i}\geq 1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}
in order to avoid a contradiction.
Similarly, we have that
\langle Q,M\rangle\leq 1-\gamma^{2}/2
, so
\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}\langle Q,z_{i}z_{i}^{\top}\rangle\leq 1-\gamma^{2}/2
.
Let
S^{\prime}=\{i\mid\langle Q,z_{i}z_{i}^{\top}\rangle\leq 1-\gamma^{2}/4\}
.
Then we must have
\sum_{i\in S^{\prime}}\lambda_{i}\geq\gamma^{2}/4
in order to avoid a contradiction. Combining these,

\sum_{i\in S\cap S^{\prime}}\lambda_{i}\geq\gamma^{2}/4-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\geq\gamma^{2}/8\,,

where we used that
\gamma^{2}\geq 8\sqrt{\varepsilon}
.
We argue now that the algorithm samples
w
with probability
1-\varepsilon^{1/4}
as some
z_{i}
with
i\in S\cap S^{\prime}
, which implies that
\lVert Pw\rVert^{2}\geq 1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}
and
\lVert Qw\rVert^{2}\leq 1-\gamma^{2}/4
.
By definition, the algorithm always samples
w
as some
z_{i}
with
i\in S^{\prime}
.
In addition,
\sum_{i\not\in S}\lambda_{i}\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}
.
Therefore, the algorithm also samples
w
as some
z_{i}
with
i\in S
with probability at least
\frac{\gamma^{2}/8}{\gamma^{2}/8+\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\geq 1-\varepsilon^{1/4}
, where we used that
\gamma^{2}\geq 8\varepsilon^{1/4}
.

Next, we prove that if at some iteration there does not exist any degree-
t
pseudo-expectation that satisfies
\mathcal{A}\cup\{\lVert Qv\rVert^{2}\leq 1-\gamma^{2}/2\}
, then the guarantees of the theorem hold.
Indeed, such a pseudo-expectation exists as long as there exists any unit vector
v
that satisfies
\mathcal{A}
and
\lVert Qv\rVert^{2}\leq 1-\gamma^{2}/2
.
If no unit vector
v
satisfies
\mathcal{A}
, then the algorithm stops at the first iteration and returns
0^{d\times d}
, which trivially satisfies the guarantees of the theorem.
