\displaystyle\quad\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{44.50748pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-44.50748pt\raisebox{-8.20114pt}{\makebox[44.50748pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(\log w_{\min}^{-1})}$}}}\hskip-44.50748pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[44.50748pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\hat{\mathcal{A}}\left(v,O\left(\left(w_{\min}^{-1}\right)^{10\log w_{\min}^{-1}}\left((w_{\min}^{-1})^{40\log w_{\min}^{-1}}\delta^{20\log w_{\min}^{-1}}+\left(w_{\min}^{-1}\right)^{40\log w_{\min}^{-1}}\gamma^{2}\right)^{1/2}\right)\right)\,.

When
\delta\leq\Omega(w_{\min}^{3}\varepsilon^{1/(10\log w_{\min}^{-1})})

and
\gamma\leq\Omega(w_{\min}^{40\log w_{\min}^{-1}}\varepsilon)
, this implies
\widehat{{\mathcal{A}}}(v,\varepsilon)
.

The claim about the monomials in the sum-of-squares proofs follows from composition of sum-of-squares proofs.
∎

6.1.4
Proof of
Theorem

6.5

Let
\tilde{\mu}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mu_{i}
and
\tilde{\Sigma}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}+\Sigma
.
Let
P
be the orthogonal projection to the subspace of eigenvectors of
\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\Sigma\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}
whose eigenvalues are at most
1-\delta^{1/16}
.
Also let
R
be the orthogonal projection to the subspace of eigenvectors of
\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\Sigma\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}
whose eigenvalues are at most
1-\delta
, where
\delta\sim w_{\min}^{3}\varepsilon^{1/(10\log w_{\min}^{-1})}
.

By
Lemma

6.15
, we can compute in time
\mathrm{poly}(d^{\log w_{\min}^{-1}},(w_{\min}^{-1})^{\log w_{\min}^{-1}},\varepsilon^{-1})
a system of
polynomial inequalities
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
of size
\mathrm{poly}(d^{\log w_{\min}^{-1}})
in indeterminate
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, including
\lVert v\rVert^{2}\leq 1
, such that

•

\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{44.50748pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-44.50748pt\raisebox{-8.20114pt}{\makebox[44.50748pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(\log w_{\min}^{-1})}$}}}\hskip-44.50748pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[44.50748pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\left\{\left\lVert Pv\right\rVert^{2}\leq O(w_{\min}^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/(80\log w_{\min}^{-1})})\right\}
,

•

\left\{\lVert v\rVert^{2}\leq 1,Rv=0\right\}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{44.50748pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-44.50748pt\raisebox{-8.20114pt}{\makebox[44.50748pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{O(\log w_{\min}^{-1})}$}}}\hskip-44.50748pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[44.50748pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}$}}}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
,

and such that each monomial
r(v)^{2}\prod_{i\in S}p_{i}(v)
in the sum-of-squares proofs has
|S|\leq O(1)
.

Because of the isotropic position, we have trivially that
\mathrm{rank}(P)\leq k
.
Then, by applying
Theorem

4.2
to
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
with orthogonal projection matrices
P
and
R
, we can compute in time
f(w_{\min}^{-1},\varepsilon^{-1})\cdot\mathrm{poly}(d^{\log^{2}w_{\min}^{-1}})
a
D
-dimensional subspace such that, for every unit vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\lVert Pv\rVert^{2}\geq 1-\alpha
, there exists a unit vector in the subspace that is
\beta
-close to it, where

D=O(w_{\min}^{1/8}\varepsilon^{-1/(640\log w_{\min}^{-1})})^{k}\,,

\alpha=\Omega(w_{\min}^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/(80\log w_{\min}^{-1})})\,,

\beta=O(w_{\min}^{-1/8}\varepsilon^{1/(640\log w_{\min}^{-1})})\,.

We argue now that the unit vectors
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\lVert Pv\rVert^{2}\geq 1-\alpha
are the ones we are interested in.
First, we have that all unit vectors
v
that lie in the subspace of eigenvectors of
\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\Sigma\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}
with eigenvalues at most
1-\delta^{1/16}
satisfy
\lVert Pv\rVert^{2}=1\geq 1-\alpha
.

Second, we want a result about
\Sigma
, not
\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\Sigma\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}
.
Note that

\displaystyle|v^{\top}\Sigma v-v^{\top}\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\Sigma\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}v|

\displaystyle=|v^{\top}(\Sigma-\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\Sigma\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2})v|\leq\lVert v\rVert^{2}\lVert\Sigma-\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\Sigma\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\rVert

\displaystyle=\lVert\Sigma-(I_{d}+E)\Sigma(I_{d}+E)\rVert\leq 2\lVert E\rVert\lVert\Sigma\rVert+\lVert E\rVert^{2}\lVert\Sigma\rVert

\displaystyle\leq O(\gamma)\,,

where we used that
E=\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}-I_{d}
satisfies
\lVert E\rVert\leq O(\gamma)
and
\lVert\Sigma\rVert\leq 1+\gamma
.
Let
P^{\prime}
be the orthogonal projection to the subspace of eigenvectors of
\Sigma
with eigenvalues at most
1/2
.
Note that all unit vectors
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
that lie in the subspace associated with
I_{d}-P
satisfy
w^{\top}\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\Sigma\tilde{\Sigma}^{-1/2}w>1-\delta^{1/16}
, so also
w^{\top}\Sigma w>1-\delta^{1/16}-O(\gamma)\geq 1-O(\delta^{1/16})
.
Then because of the
\gamma
-approximate isotropic position we get for all such unit vectors
w
that

1-O(\delta^{1/16})\leq w^{\top}\Sigma w\leq(1/2)\lVert P^{\prime}w\rVert^{2}+(1+\gamma)(1-\lVert P^{\prime}w\rVert^{2})\,,

so
\lVert P^{\prime}w\rVert^{2}\leq O(\delta^{1/16})+O(\gamma)\leq O(\delta^{1/16})
.
This implies that each unit vector
v
with
\lVert P^{\prime}v\rVert^{2}=1
has squared inner product at most
O(\delta^{1/16})
with any unit vector in
I_{d}-P
, so it has squared inner product at least
1-O(\delta^{1/16})
with some unit vector in
P
, so it has distance at most
O(\delta^{1/32})
from some unit vector in
P
.
Therefore
v
is
(\beta+O(\delta^{1/32}))
-close to a unit vector in the returned subspace, which is dominated by
O(\beta)
.

∎

6.2
Algorithm for Clustering Non-Spherical Mixtures with Identical Unknown Covariance

We now present the clustering algorithm for mixtures whose components have an identical unknown covariance.

Algorithm 4

Mixtures of identical-covariance Gaussians learning algorithm

input :
a collection of samples
\mathcal{T}
, the number of components
k
, the minimum mixing weight
w_{\min}
, the separation parameter
\Delta

output :
a clustering
\mathcal{S}
of
\mathcal{T}

1

Partition
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\mathcal{T}_{2}
by putting each sample in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
independently with probability
1/2
\the\algocf@printnl@i{{{3}}}footnote
\the\algocf@printnl@i{{{3}}}footnote
\algocf@printnl@i{{{3}}}footnote:

\the\algocf@printnl@i{{{3}}}footnote

4

This increases the fraction of outliers in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
with high probability by at most a constant factor.
;

2

Let
\mathcal{C}=\left\{\mathcal{S}\right\}
where
\mathcal{S}
is the trivial partial clustering with one subset
\mathcal{T}_{1}
;

3

while

\exists\,\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{C}
such that
|\mathcal{S}|<k

do

4

Remove
\mathcal{S}
from
\mathcal{C}
;

5

Let
L
be the output of the partial clustering refinement algorithm (
Algorithm

5
) on
\mathcal{S}
;

6

Add all
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\in L
to
\mathcal{C}
;

7

end while

8

Run the clustering selection algorithm (
Lemma

A.1
) on
\mathcal{C}
with upper bound
\Delta^{-1/(\log w_{\min}^{-1})^{2k}}
on the fraction of corruptions and with new samples
\mathcal{T}_{2}
, and let
\mathcal{S}
be the returned clustering;

9

return

\mathcal{S}
;

Algorithm 5

Mixtures of identical-covariance Gaussians partial clustering refinement algorithm

input :
a candidate partial clustering
\mathcal{S}
, the number of components
k
, the minimum mixing weight
w_{\min}
, the separation parameter
\Delta

output :
a list of candidate refinements of
\mathcal{S}

1

Let
L=\varnothing
;

2

for

each
S\in\mathcal{S}
and each guess of
\varepsilon
with
2^{-\Delta}
-bit complexity that satisfies
\varepsilon\geq\Delta^{-1}

do

3

Put the samples of
S
in isotropic position with the algorithm corresponding to
Fact

3.21
;

4

Let
Q
be the subspace returned by the same-covariance subspace recovery algorithm (
Theorem

6.5
) run on
S
with fraction of outliers set to
\varepsilon
(but abort and set
Q=\emptyset
if the algorithm performs more than
f(\Delta)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(d^{\log^{2}w_{\min}^{-1}})
steps for some function
f
);

5

If
\dim(Q)>f(\Delta)
for some function
f
, set
Q=\emptyset
;

6

for

each unit vector
v
in a
\Delta^{-1}
-net of
Q

do

7

for

each
1/(100k)
-resolution choice of
\tau
in
[-2w_{\min}^{-1/2},2w_{\min}^{-1/2}]

do

8

Partition the samples
S=S_{1}\cup S_{2}
based on whether their projection on
v
is in the interval
(-\infty,\tau]
or outside it;

9

Add
(\mathcal{S}\setminus S)\cup\{S_{1},S_{2}\}
to
L
;

10

end for

11

end for

12

end for

13

return

L
;

6.2.1
Analysis of Partial Clustering Refinement (
Algorithm

5
)

We now show that the refinement subroutine has the desired properties.

Lemma 6.16

(Mean clustering result)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a
(1-\varepsilon)
-good partial clustering.
Suppose there exists some
S\in\mathcal{S}
with
|S|\geq\mathrm{poly}(d,\Delta)
that contains at least two components, and suppose that the mixture corresponding to components in
\mathsf{comp}(S)
is in approximate isotropic position:
\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{k}w^{\prime}_{i}\mu_{i}\right\rVert\leq\varepsilon^{2}
and
(1-\varepsilon^{2})I_{d}\preceq\sum_{i\in\mathsf{comp}(S)}w^{\prime}_{i}\mu_{i}\mu_{i}^{\top}+\Sigma\preceq(1+\varepsilon^{2})I_{d}
, where
w^{\prime}_{i}=\frac{w_{i}}{\sum_{i\in\mathsf{comp}(S)}w_{i}}
.
Then for
\Delta^{-1}\leq\varepsilon\leq w_{\min}^{O(\log w_{\min}^{-1})}
the output of
Algorithm

5
contains with high probability some
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
that is a
(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})
-good partial clustering for
\varepsilon^{\prime}=O(w_{\min}^{-4}\varepsilon^{1/(640\log w_{\min}^{-1})})
.

Proof.

Consider the iteration of the loop in which we select a cluster
S
and a value
\varepsilon
that correspond to the assumptions.
We show that with high probability in this case the inner loop will produce a
(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})
-good refinement of
\mathcal{S}
.

Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
be the means of any two components in
\mathsf{comp}(S)
, and let
v
be a direction in which they are separated, i.e.,

\langle\mu_{1}-\mu_{2},v\rangle^{2}\geq\Delta^{2}v^{\top}\Sigma v\,.

By the approximate isotropic position, we have that
\langle\mu_{1},v\rangle^{2},\langle\mu_{2},v\rangle^{2}\leq 2w_{\min}^{-1}
, and therefore
v^{\top}\Sigma v\leq O(w_{\min}^{-1}\Delta^{-2})
.

Because
v^{\top}\Sigma v\leq O(w_{\min}^{-1}\Delta^{-2})
, we have that
v
is
O(w_{\min}^{-1/2}\Delta^{-1})
-close to the subspace of eigenvectors of
\Sigma
with eigenvalues at most
1/2
.
Then when we run the subspace recovery algorithm in
Theorem

6.5
, we obtain with high probability a subspace that contains a unit vector that is
O(w_{\min}^{-1/2}\Delta^{-1}+w_{\min}^{-1/8}\varepsilon^{1/(640\log w_{\min}^{-1})})
-close to
v
, which is dominated by
O(w_{\min}^{-1/8}\varepsilon^{1/(640\log w_{\min}^{-1})})
.
Then there also exists a unit vector
v^{\prime}
in the
\Delta^{-1}
-net that is
O(w_{\min}^{-1/8}\varepsilon^{1/(640\log w_{\min}^{-1})})
-close to
v
.
For this
v^{\prime}
, we have by
Claim

5.22
that
(v^{\prime})^{\top}\Sigma v^{\prime}\leq O(w_{\min}^{-1/8}\varepsilon^{1/(640\log w_{\min}^{-1})})
.

We argue now that in direction
v^{\prime}
there exist two means that are
\Omega(1)
-far.
We have because of the approximate isotropic position that

\sum_{i\in\mathsf{comp}(S)}w_{i}^{\prime}\langle\mu_{i},v^{\prime}\rangle^{2}+(v^{\prime})^{\top}\Sigma v^{\prime}\geq 1-\varepsilon^{2}\,,

so there exists some
i\in\mathsf{comp}(S)
such that
|\langle\mu_{i},v^{\prime}\rangle|\geq 0.9
. On the other hand,
\sum_{i\in\mathsf{comp}(S)}w_{i}^{\prime}\langle\mu_{i},v^{\prime}\rangle\leq\varepsilon^{2}\leq w_{\min}^{100}
, so there also exists some
j\in\mathsf{comp}(S)
such that
\langle\mu_{i},v^{\prime}\rangle\langle\mu_{j},v^{\prime}\rangle\leq 0
, because otherwise the contribution of
\langle\mu_{i},v^{\prime}\rangle
to the sum would be too large. Overall, this implies that
|\langle\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},v^{\prime}\rangle|\geq 0.9
.

Then, if we sort all components in increasing order by their means in direction
v^{\prime}
, there exist two consecutive indices
i
and
i+1
such that
|\langle\mu_{i+1}-\mu_{i},v^{\prime}\rangle|\geq 1/(2k)
.
Also recall that
(v^{\prime})^{\top}\Sigma(v^{\prime})\leq O(w_{\min}^{-1/8}\varepsilon^{1/(640\log w_{\min}^{-1})})
.
Consider partitioning the samples projected along direction
v^{\prime}
with the threshold
\tau=\frac{\langle\mu_{i},v^{\prime}\rangle+\langle\mu_{i+1},v^{\prime}\rangle}{2}
. Then we have by Chebyshev’s inequality that an independent sample from the mixture is partitioned erroneously with independent probability at most
O(w_{\min}^{-3}\varepsilon^{1/(640\log w_{\min}^{-1})})
.

Then, by Hoeffding’s inequality, we have that with probability
\exp\left(-|S|\cdot O(w_{\min}^{-6}\varepsilon^{1/(320\log w_{\min}^{-1})})\right)
at most an
O(w_{\min}^{-3}\varepsilon^{1/(640\log w_{\min}^{-1})})
-fraction of the samples are partitioned erroneously.
In the worst case in which one part contains only one component with weight
w_{\min}
, the fraction of outliers in it is at most
O(w_{\min}^{-4}\varepsilon^{1/(640\log w_{\min}^{-1})})
.

A problem we omitted is that the bound on the probability that a sample is partitioned erroneously holds for a fixed direction independent of the samples, but
v^{\prime}
can depend on the samples.
We can show however by a union bound that the result holds for all
v^{\prime}
.
Consider a
\mathrm{poly}(d^{-1},\Delta^{-1})
-net of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which has size
\mathrm{poly}(d,\Delta)^{d}
.
The probability that a sample is partitioned erroneously in a direction that is
\mathrm{poly}(d^{-1},\Delta^{-1})
-close to
v^{\prime}
is the same up to constants to the probability for direction
v^{\prime}
, so the bound by Hoeffding’s inequality is also essentially the same.
Then to union bound over all the directions in the net it suffices to have
|S|\geq\mathrm{poly}(d,\Delta)
.

Furthermore, we note that we obtain essentially the same probability of partitioning a sample erroneously even if the threshold is within an additive distance of
1/(100k)
from the threshold
\tau
we analyzed.
Also note that the threshold is trivially upper bounded by
2w_{\min}^{-1/2}
, because
|\langle\mu_{i},v\rangle|\leq 2w_{\min}^{-1/2}
for all
i\in[k]
.

Thus, when we loop over every unit vector in the net, we find a direction
v^{\prime}
for which we produce a
(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})
-good partial clustering for
\varepsilon^{\prime}=O(w_{\min}^{-4}\varepsilon^{1/(640\log w_{\min}^{-1})})
.

Finally, we note that we do not need the exact value of
\varepsilon
and an upper bound within
2^{-\Delta}
of the truth suffices.
∎

We also show that the refinement procedure produces a list of dimension-independent size.

Lemma 6.17

(Partial clustering refinement produces a small list)
.

For all partitions
\mathcal{S}
of the sample universe such that
|\mathcal{S}|<k
, the size of the list returned by
Algorithm

5
has size at most
f(w_{\min}^{-1},\Delta)
for some function
f
.

Proof.

We first consider the number of candidate refinements added to the list in a single iteration of the loop.
We run the identical-covariance mixture subspace recovery algorithm and for each
v
in a
\Delta^{-1}
-net of the outputted subspace we add at most
\mathrm{poly}(w_{\min})
possible refinements to the output list.
The dimension of the subspace outputted by the identical-covariance mixture subspace recovery algorithm in
Theorem

6.5
is at most some function of
\Delta
.
Then, the size of the
\Delta^{-1}
-net of the subspace is also bounded by some function of
\Delta
.
Finally, the number of iterations of the loop is bounded by a function exponential in
\Delta
.
∎

Lemma 6.18

(Correctness of partial clustering refinement)
.

Given a partial clustering
\mathcal{S}
with
|\mathcal{S}|<k
,
Algorithm

5
outputs a list of partial clusterings
L
with the following properties:

1.

Every
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\in L
is a refinement of
\mathcal{S}
,

2.

The size of
L
is bounded by
f(w_{\min}^{-1},\Delta)
for some function
f
,

3.

If
\mathcal{S}
is a
(1-\varepsilon)
-good partial clustering for
\Delta^{-1}\leq\varepsilon\leq w_{\min}^{O(\log w_{\min}^{-1})}
with
|S|\geq\mathrm{poly}(d,\Delta)
for all
S\in\mathcal{S}
, then the output of the algorithm contains with high probability at least one refinement
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
that is a
(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})
-good clustering with
\varepsilon^{\prime}=O(w_{\min}^{-4}\varepsilon^{1/(6000\log w_{\min}^{-1})})
.

Proof.

The first property is immediate from the definition of the algorithm. The second property follows by
Lemma

6.17
.

For the rest of the proof, suppose that
\mathcal{S}
is a
(1-\varepsilon)
-good partial clustering with
|S|\geq\mathrm{poly}(d,\Delta)
for all
S\in\mathcal{S}
.

The robust isotropic position algorithm corresponding to
Fact

3.21
succeeds with high probability and we can assume the mixture is in approximate isotropic position, with new mixture mean and covariance
{\left\lVert\mu^{\prime}\right\rVert\leq\varepsilon^{1/4}}
,
{(1-\varepsilon^{1/4})I_{d}\preceq\Sigma^{\prime}\preceq(1+\varepsilon^{1/4})I_{d}}
, and
{\left\lVert\Sigma^{\prime}-I_{d}\right\rVert_{F}\leq\varepsilon^{1/4}}
.

Because the size of
\mathcal{S}
is less than
k
, for some cluster
S\in\mathcal{S}
we have
|\mathsf{comp}(S)|>1
.
Recall that all the components are
\Delta
-separated, so for such a cluster with more than one component each
i\neq j\in\mathsf{comp}(S)
have means that are
\Delta
-separated.

Then we apply
Lemma

6.16
with the assumption that we have a
(1-\varepsilon^{1/8})
-good partial clustering (so that the approximate isotropic position requirement of
Lemma

6.16
is satisfied), and conclude that with high probability the outputted list contains a
{(1-O(w_{\min}^{-4}\varepsilon^{1/(6000\log w_{\min}^{-1})}))}
-good clustering.
∎

6.2.2
Proof of
Theorem

6.2

As in the proof of
Theorem

5.2
, all partial clusterings that are contained in
\mathcal{C}
in
Algorithm

4
at any point are associated with a tree where the children of a partial clustering
\mathcal{S}
are the partial clustering
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
produced when we run the refinement algorithm on
\mathcal{S}
.
The number of clusters increases by one at each level and is bounded by
k
and thus the depth is at most
k
.

For correctness, note that by
Lemma

6.18
with high probability this tree contains at least one path from the root to a leaf such at each level if the node is a
(1-\varepsilon)
-good partial clustering with
\varepsilon\geq\Delta^{-1}
then the next node in the path is an
(1-\varepsilon^{\prime})
-good partial clustering with
\varepsilon^{\prime}=O(w_{\min}^{-4}\varepsilon^{1/(6000\log w_{\min}^{-1})})
. Conditioning on the existence of a path from the root to level
i
, we can extend the path to level
i+1
with high probability by
Lemma

6.18
.
By taking a union bound over the
k-1
steps of
Algorithm

5
needed to extend the path from the root to the leaves, we have that this path will exist with high probability.
We can assume we start with
\varepsilon=\max\left\{O(\varepsilon^{*}),\Delta^{-1}\right\}
, such that with high probability the samples in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
have at most an
\varepsilon
-fraction of outliers.
Then, we have that at level
i
the partial clustering is a
\left(1-O\left(w_{\min}^{-5}\varepsilon^{1/(6000\log w_{\min}^{-1})^{i}}\right)\right)
-good clustering when
\varepsilon^{-1}\geq f(w_{\min}^{-1})
is large enough, which we lower bound by
1-\varepsilon^{1/(\log w_{\min}^{-1})^{2k}}
for
i\leq k
.

Thus for
(\varepsilon^{*})^{-1}\geq f(w_{\min}^{-1})
large enough there exists some leaf that corresponds to a candidate clustering in the final list
\mathcal{C}
with few outliers, as required by
Lemma

A.1
.
Then by
Lemma

A.1
we have that we output a
(1-O(kw_{\min}^{-1}\max\{\varepsilon,\Delta^{-\Omega(1/k)}\}))
-good clustering of the input samples.

The argument for the time complexity is similar to that of the proof of
Theorem

5.2
, with the difference that now the subspace finding algorithm takes time
g(w_{\min}^{-1},\Delta)\cdot\mathrm{poly}(d^{\log^{2}w_{\min}^{-1}})
for some function
g
.

∎

Appendix A
Clustering Selection

We describe now an algorithm that takes input a list of clusterings such that one of them is “good” and outputs a good clustering.

Algorithm 6

Clustering selection algorithm

input :
a list of candidate clusterings
\mathcal{C}
containing one good clustering, the number of components
k
, the minimum mixing weight
w_{\min}
, an upper bound
\varepsilon
on the fraction of corruptions in the best clustering, and a set of new samples from the original distribution such that the samples are independent of the list of candidate clusterings

output :
a clustering
\mathcal{S}
of all samples in the input

1

for

each candidate clustering
\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{C}

do

2

for

each
S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}

do

3

Run the Gaussian estimation algorithm in
Fact

3.18
on
S_{i}
with fraction of outliers
\varepsilon
to produce estimates
\hat{\mu}_{i}
and
\hat{\Sigma}_{i}
;

4

Estimate
\hat{w}_{i}
as
|S_{i}|/\sum_{S\in\mathcal{S}}|S|
;

5

end for

6

Let
M_{\mathcal{S}}=\sum_{S_{i}\in\mathcal{S}}\hat{w}_{i}N(\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i})
;

7

end for

8

Run the tournament algorithm in
Fact

3.23
on the distributions
M_{\mathcal{S}}
for all clusterings
\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{C}
, given the set of new samples;

9

Let
\hat{M}
be the winning distribution in the tournament;

10

Cluster all samples in the input into a clustering
\mathcal{S}
using the parameters of
\hat{M}
, as in the proof of Theorem 9.3 of
[
32
]
;

11

return

\mathcal{S}

Lemma A.1

(Clustering selection)
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a mixture
\sum_{i=1}^{k}w_{i}N(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
with
w_{\min}=\min_{i}w_{i}
.
Assume all components
i\neq j
are
\Delta
-separated with
\Delta\geq f(w_{\min}^{-1})
for some function
f
.
Also let
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
be a distribution satisfying
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathcal{D})\leq\varepsilon^{*}
.
Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a list of
m
candidate clusterings of
n
i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
Suppose that all clusterings in
{\mathcal{C}}
have size
k
and that there exists a clustering
\mathcal{S}\in{\mathcal{C}}
that is
(1-\varepsilon)
-good.
Suppose you are also given an
\mathrm{poly}(m,\varepsilon^{-1})
new i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
.
Suppose
\varepsilon^{*},\varepsilon\leq g(w_{\min})
for some function
g
.
Then, for
n\geq\mathrm{poly}(d,w_{\min}^{-1},\varepsilon^{-1})
,
Algorithm

6
outputs with high probability a
(1-O\left(kw_{\min}^{-1}\max\{\varepsilon^{*},\Delta^{-\Omega(1/k)}\})\right)
-good clustering of all
n+\mathrm{poly}(m,\varepsilon^{-1})
samples.

Proof.

Consider the clustering
\mathcal{S}\in{\mathcal{C}}
that is
(1-\varepsilon)
-good.
Let
\mathcal{S}=\left\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{k}\right\}
.
Because
\mathcal{S}
is
(1-\varepsilon)
-good, for each
S_{i}
we have that
|\mathsf{comp}(S_{i})|=1
. Denote by
N(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
the Gaussian distribution corresponding to the component in
S_{i}
.
Then the points in
S_{i}
can be considered an
O(\varepsilon)
-corrupted set of i.i.d. samples from
N(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i})
.
Then
Fact

3.18
guarantees that for each
S_{i}
the algorithm recovers some
\hat{\mu}_{i}
and
\hat{\Sigma}_{i}
such that

d_{\mathrm{TV}}(N(\mu_{i},\Sigma_{i}),N(\hat{\mu}_{i},\hat{\Sigma}_{i}))\leq O(\varepsilon\log^{3/2}(1/\varepsilon))\,.

We also have by standard concentration bounds that, as long as the number of samples is a large enough polynomial in
w_{\min}^{-1}
, with high probability
|w_{i}-\hat{w}_{i}|\leq O(\varepsilon)
.
Then the total variation distance between
M_{\mathcal{S}}
and the ground truth mixture is at most
O(\varepsilon\log^{3/2}(1/\varepsilon))
.

Applying the hypothesis selection algorithm in
Fact

3.23
on the hypotheses
M_{\mathcal{S}}
computed for all clusterings
\mathcal{S}\in{\mathcal{C}}
, we obtain with high probability some distribution
\hat{M}
such that the total variation distance to the ground truth mixture is at most
O(\varepsilon\log^{3/2}(1/\varepsilon))
.

The parameter separation of the components of the ground truth also implies that the total variation distance between these components is, say, at least constant — otherwise it would be
information-theoretically
impossible to cluster the mixture, but we know that clustering is possible, e.g., because of the algorithm of
[
6
]
.
Then by
Fact

3.19
, for each component
\hat{G}_{i}
of
\hat{M}
there exists a component
G_{i}
of the ground truth such that
d_{\mathrm{TV}}(G_{i},\hat{G}_{i})\leq\mathrm{poly}_{k}(\varepsilon)
.
This implies that
\hat{G}_{i}
and
G_{i}
also have parameter separation upper bounded by, say, an absolute constant (otherwise they could not be close in total variation distance).
Then clustering can be achieved by standard robust clustering techniques: see the proof of Theorem 9.3 in
[
32
]
, assuming that a good set of
k
parameters already exists. Note that their analysis implicitly assumes that, roughly,
\varepsilon^{*}\geq\Delta^{-\Omega(1/k)}
, and we account for this in our guarantee.
∎
